ALWAYS USE NITOTJG.

LADIES' LETTER. U y DEAR NELLIE,

lam of the opinion that light chips and ulna will be much used in the early Spring millinery, and the soft satin braids of last year, as they are so adaptable to the wonderful twisting and twirl- ing which the fashionable toque has to undergo. Indeed, more in the fancifulness of the shape than M the trimming lies the style of the headgear of she moment. The toque, having enlarged its bor- ders and spread its wings, now in virtue of its in- creased size is dubbed a touquet, but the neater toque is still dear to the heart of many a well. woman, who knows what best suits her face and style of hairdressing, and remains faithful bo her ideal. Coloured vests and collars are much worn now, and don't look at all patchy if properly managed. A dark or medium colouring should have a collar and full vest of the new cerise satin, the vest having three-quarter-iuch black lace inser- tion applied lattice fashion, and the collar trimmed in a similar manner, and edged also with narrower insertion. If this sounds like too much colour, have a cream vest, with black lace in the same fashion, or with groups of five tucks set across or downwards, according as you are slender or the reverse, alternating with stripes of the black in sertion.

ft really seemed that black cloth gowns have been rather out of fashion during the early winter, but a reaction seems setting in in their favour, and some exceedingly stylish models are 11view. One has the skirt-seams marked by bands of

stitched cloth terminating in points 'resting on a small foot-frill of the cloth edged with waved black silk braid, while the very smart Eton coat is trimmed with braid and-strappings, and has bell- sleeves showing under ones of deep rose-red glace, , the vest being of the same. The latter is finished -by a long-ended sash of black satin. There is a ,pretty muff of black velvet, chinchilla, and•rose.red glace to accompany the costume, and a large turban-like toque to correspond. knotherattrac- tive black gown is trimmed with stitched bands of black panne, six or seven edging the alrirt and nearly as many striping the full fronts of the bodice on either side of a pointed vest of black silk covered with cream lace applique. These stitched bands of cloth, panne or glace, are indeed the most popular trimming for all walking dresses, and are only rivalled by the fine tucks-and eordings. Pale grey cloth gowns trimmed with bands of panne look very smart for ceremonions occasions, the fashionable shade being an opalescent bluish tint. The Princess() polonaise is once again, in fashion, as is the perfectly tight bodice which is so becoming to a good figure. The latter, however, is always relieved from absolute plainness by a fur collar or a lace cravat of some kind, and the buttons are invariably smart ones of silver or enamel.

the other day two charming blouses. One cream-tinted guipure over silk. It is made over carefully fitted, well-boned lining, and has none that superfluous fulness at the waist which makes so many blouse-bodices look unfinished and untidy. The shaped collar is in panneof a reddish many() colour, and bands of similar panne encircle the sleeves above the elbows. The folded waist. belt is also made of panne. Silk fringe borders the collar. T‘.e full vest is made of silk muslin, and the puffs of the sleeves match it. The neckband and undersleeves are lace sewn with silver sequins. The other blouse is black silk with revers faced with fancy embroidery on cream-coloured canvas. The fulness is drawn down under a band of the same, and the collar is also in the embroidery.

4ck plush is a geei deal seen just now in warm Ater capes and jackets. Many of these are .ichly braided or embroidered, and have fu: r jet trimmings on the collar and revers or round the edge. Some have the sack back, either plain or with guipure or jet across the shoulders, trimming - match ornamenting the front. Jet is not so meh wo-n as itws. no dresses that are a mass of celluloid sequins constitute a real danger, not only to the wearer herself, but to every one lightly dre6sed who is in her vicinity. The celluloid is highly inflammable, and a spark from a cigarette or a too close pro,:imity to a fire or a gas jet may be quite enough to cause a terrible accident, with results of the grimmest kind. Let every one who possesses a drum of the kind take off a sequin or two from an under seam and experiment with these. If a lighted match set to them cause no ignition or smouldering, then all is safe. But should the sequins catch fire or even smoulder, the experi- menter can readily picture for herself the con- dition of her arms should a pair of sequined sleeves catch fire. The scare about the cheap, inflammable, imitation tortoise-shell combs seems to have ended, but it is to be feared that numbers of these combs and slides are still sold.

Your loving, JuNntis.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Few among us can realise as yet, that our beloved Queen has left us, that her long and glorious reign is really at an end. To the great majority,—who have known no other Sovereign,—it will seem sadly strange that a time could be when Her Majesty had no part in the daily round. But in our hearts our Queen can never die. the Qusea we have reverenced, and loved so long. Osborne House where Her Majesty passed to her rest, is of all the Royal residences the most comfortable. There is nothing about it of the vastness of Windsor,and on the other hand, there is no cramping a room as at , Balmoral. Very soon after her marriage. the Que?e made up her mind that she must have some home of tier I very owe, where beneath the shade of clustering trees, and accompanied only by the song of birds, she could cast aside the splendours of court life, forget for a time Prime Ministers and Privy Counsellors, and give full vent to that tender love for her husband, and that maternal solicitude for her young family which have been the two most beautiful traits in a character ever womanly and pure. That her Marety was more than satisfied with her marine home is fully set forth in a letter written soon after acquiring it, to her uncle Leopold. King of the Bel- giana, wnich ran as follows:—" It is impossible to see a prettier place, with woods, and valleys, and points of view that would be beautiful anywhere but when coin— I bleed with the sea (to which the woods grow down to a beach which is quite private) it is really everything you I could wish.''—ln tbis peaceful restful spot, our Sover-- eign spent, what has proved to be, the last Christmas of her life, a spot which—the tut sad joerney Windsor accomplished—can know her no more. From the earliest part of her reign,the Queen showed a determination to keep in her own hands, the appoint- ment of those serving in her households. Much devoted attention did Her MajEety give to the selection of these individuals,with the result that she was always ex- cellently served. Never would the Queen permit in- solence to a servant, and often has she rebuked her grandchildren and great-grandchildren for any want of civility to their attendants. In this,as in so many other divers ways,we should do well to follow in Her Majesty's footsteps. Courtesy to servants should early be in- stilled into the minds of the little folk!, and the least incivility, or want of consiaeration immediatly checked. Rare indeed is the individual, seen abroad, who has not vet paid the last respect, possible to our lamented Queen and donned mourning. That the supply of black ebould have been equal to the demand is little short of marvellous when we take into consideration tha huge dimensions of that demand. With regard to the London season, this of course will 13,-.t be passible, and the many lovely " creations " already completed, or in course of construction, for the Eirly Drawing Room., wit!, alas, not now be ruseded. Sombre indeed is our attire just now, a state ot affairs which blends exactly with our

feelings. Few people nowadays dress their children in black possibly following the fathion set t.y the Duchess of York, wh ha, never allowed her little ones to he so garbed, deciding when in maiming, for white only. About the wiFdom of such a precedure there can be no possible d,ubt. A child ore.sed in black is a sorrowful sight, and sorrow and the little tolks should ever be kept as far apart as possible. The clouds must we know come later, we should therefore see to it that the sun- shine at last outlasts childhood's days. Many modistes nowadays make a speciality of chil- dren's clothes, an item to be thankful for, as the ordinary shop sins horribly in dribbling little trimmings about a child's frock, making it fussy without being fit, and giving an air of vulgarity to the nicest little girl. There should be nothing ineonssquent about a child's frock, i: should entirely appeal to our sense of fitness, which it may do if rightly designed, without a suspicion of being uninteresting and ugly. The huge sash has !almost' disappeared, the soft-fringed variety now—

_ . happily—reigning in its stead. Large picture hats—hlack of 'course, have again re- turned to favour. Generally speaking they are eminently becoming, very picturesque, and in common with most black things, have a happy knack of not dat- ing from the day they are bought. Millinery is unques- tionably growing less costly as the years go on. Many leading firms have for some time turned out effective ter:lnm and bats at considerably below the sum that would have been demanded a few seasons back, with the result that they are doing an immense business In thei-i I lava vriiere. !a I be seen wearing the same hat or toque more than a very few times is looked upon almost as a social crime, few care to pay for headgear the figure they were wont to, when change and variation was not quite so much the order of the day as it is now. The marriages of American millionaires are usually resplendent affairs. The Vanderbilt wedding at New- port the other day was as brilliant and as unostenta- tious, as a vulgar display of unlimited wealth could make it, but there are occasional exceptions to these magnificent parades. An example was afforded by the marriage of Miss Alta Rockefeilet, to Mr. Pannalee Pren- tice, which was quietly celebrated at the house of her fa‘her in New York, a few days ago. All that Mr. John D. Rockefeller stipulated for was that his son-in-law should be a man of good character, and Mr. Prentice is described as a young barrister, and Sunday school worker, whose share in this world's gods is estimated at £2.000. Elia wife comes to him with £10,000,000, whicb after all, is a mere bagatelle to her father. Mr. John D. Rockeieller, who is s xtv-ooe years of age, is known to make an income of several millions annually, and there Is little doubt that he is more wealthy than all the Rothschild's out together. Spring-cleaning. while you wait, is one of the latest devices from America for simplifying household manage- ment. All that is necessary, is, to apply to the company who will at once send in a competent staff of worsen .and workman, to clean, scrub, polish, and renovate every thing, in the shortest possible time. Instead of dragging on for weelo, turning out first this room and then that, the whole thing will be over in a day or two, andthe house will once more become on's on. There idea certainly has attractions, but we are perhaps scarcely prepared for it, as yet, in this country. The British housewife would hardy like to trust the overhauling of he, pOSREPISICIR3 to strangers, howeve reliable they might be,—and there are many who really delight in a period of household chaos. which is familiar most Penis under the name of "spring-cleaning." Si 9 the idea is all part of the tendency of the times, of which another Px ,mple is afforded by the pr'jct for ueiverng ready cooked dinners at houses,just the same as the baker and the milkman leave their supplies. It is in these directions that people will look for the solu- tion of the difficulties caused by the eveii-increasing trouble with servants, and the arrogance of cooka, aid there its liitla doubt shall witness such develop- iii i.he near at.ure.

DEATH OF SERGEANT PRICE AT

ORFORD BARRACKS.

"A Gallant Soldier."

It is with regret that we record the death of Superior Barrack Sergeant Price, who peacefully gassed away at Orford Barracks, Warrington, last week. He was 64 years :of age, and he gave 47 years of his life to the service of Her Majesty. During that long term of service, it is a remarkable fact that he only applied for sick leave on one oc- casion, and that was so recent as on Thursday of last week. He died before the sick leave wes re- ceived. For 19 years he had been stationed at Or- ford Barracks, and as he was actively associated with several temperance and religious institutions his figure was weil-known in the town. He was a sidesman at St George's Mission, one of the mana- gers of the parochial schools, a Good Tem- pler and active worker for the Temper- ance cause. He held the Mutiny Medal with two clasps, the medal for meritor- ious service together with an annuity for meritorious service. In India,he served under Lord Wolseley(then Captain Wolseley)and in later years correspondence frequently passed between them. Lord Wolseley on being informed of the death of Sergeant Price, wrote a sympathetic letter to the deceased sergeant's relatives in which he says Sergeant Price was " a gallant soldier, an upright and honest man, upon whom, when I was on active service, 1 could always depend." He enlisted in the 88th Regiment of foot (Con- naught Rangers) at Limerick on September sth 1855, but in the following year he transferred to the 90th Light Infantry (now 2nd Scottish Rifles). After serving at home and in Malta he embarked for China with his regiment.on board the "Transit at Portsmouth, April 6th, 1857. The disasters which this vessel met with culminated in her total loss on a coral reef in the Straits of Sunda on July 9th,1857. The whole of the men spent the next 14 days on the island of Berea and were then taken to Singa- pore. On arriving here they were ordered to pro- c,-ed to India where the Mutiny had broken out. They landed at Chinsurah in Bengal on August 12th, 1857, and at once proceeded up country to join General Havelock's force then marching to relieve the Presidency. On arriving at Lucknow a portion of the 90th, in- cluding the late Superior Barrack Sergeant Price were placed under the command of Major Mclntyre with orders to protect the wounded and the luggage, lof the army in the Alumbagh. The men afterwards joined Sir Colin Campbell's force for the final cap- ture of Lucknow and were present at the subse- quent operations in Oudh. Among the actions in which the late Sergeant Price took part may be mentioned Busserutgunge, September 21st, 1857; 'assault of the Delkoosha Palace, Seraghoose, October 19th, 1857, and Fetteghus on January 3rd, 1858. He was also a member of the storming party that recaptured the 32nd Mess House. So heavy was the fire on this place after its capture that the flag of the 90th planted by Captain Wolseley,until re- cently the Commander-in-chief of the British forces, was twice shot away. For his services in India, Superior Barrack Sergeant Price received the India medal with two clasps and was allowed to Count one year's additional service under a General Order issued 2nd June, 1862 He left India in 1869 and landed at Ports mouth on November 3rd in that year, having been away from England just over 12 years. Having received the medal and gratuity for " long service and good conduct " he was discharged from the 90th as Colour-Sergeant in September, 1875, and joined the Barrack Department of the Army Service Corps in December of that year After spending two years in the North Camp at Alders'oot and live years in Wrexham he was ordered to Orford Barracks in January, 1882, and took charge of the Army Service Corps Stores. He con- tinued to discharge these duties until the last In _

1896 he received an intimation from the Seer etary of State for War to the effect that he had been awarded a medal and annuity for" long and highly meritorious service, including the Indian Mutiny ,Campaign."

vYarrington Man's Adventure at Runcorn.— Committed fol Three Monhts.

- At the Runcorn Police Court, on Friday, before Mr. W Handley (in the chair), and Mr. Edwin thliPol3, Henry flankinson,who hails from Warring- ton, and has been several times convicted for horse and cattle stealing, and has served term of penal servitude, wns charged with fr(quenting Runcorn Docks on Wednesday night, and tailing to give a good account of himself.—llenry Moores, canal Constable, stated that he was on duty in the neigh- bourhood of Runcorn Docks, about 11 p.m. on Wednesday, when be saw the prisoner near the Tidal Dock. Upon observing witness the prisoner concealed himself behind the steam crane, and his movements generally were of a suspicious character Witness went and asked him what he was doing there, and be said lae had come down there to assist to hnul the boats up the locks. RUBY LOVEDAY.

BY SILAS K. HOCKING,

Anther of "The Strange Adventures of Israel rendray, " "The Day of Recompense," "God's Outcast," "In Spite of Fate," "Her Benny," Sm.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: The Lovedays, rich strangers from London, take fur- nished apartments at Endilloe Farm, tenanted by the Pentyres, a half-ruined family. Young Justin Pentyre is discontented at the prospect of their poverty being observed, and endeavours unsuccess- fully to persuade his people to refuse the offer. The Lovedays prove to be very agreeable persons, but Justin fails for several days to catch sight of their beautiful daughter Ruby. He returns one night, however, to find that she is missing from the farm. They fear she has fallen down a disused mine, and Justin volunteers to go down. Meanwhile, Ruby is lying at the bottom of the cliffs on the seashore, unconscious, and with a sprained ankle, which caused her to fall over the side.

CHAPTER VII. ALLOT "Life wears another face When love awakes." rieite of the law of gravitation, Justin climbed the rope much more rapidly than he descended it. A dead sheep, which he found at the bottom of the shaft, was not particularly agreeable company. And, satisfying himself that nothing else that ever possessed life was there, he hurried up the rope, hand over hand, with an alacrity that was an astonishment to himself. He was fairly well exhausted by the time he pulled himself up between the two ladders, and for awhile he sat quite still and breathed hard. He had answered from the bottom the one ques- tion that had been shouted to him, and now his companions stood round him in a circle and waited for him to speak. When he had recovered his breath, he crept along the ladder until he reached terra firma, then he walked slowly down the bank, and was quickly followed by his father and all the others. Nathan broke the silence. Nathan was quite indignant that so much labour and effort had been spent for nothing. "What can 'ee expect of people brought up in sich a way ? " he said, in a tone of scorn; and he walked two or three paces towards Endilloe by way of emphasising his remark. "It is possible she may still be alive," Justin said, without heeding Nathan. "Amos Blue saw her in the turnip field going towards the river." "But we've searched the river already," said dir•Jhn Pentyre. "Not thoroughly," Justin answered. "Go as far as Tregarrick Wood. I'll follow as soon as I'm rested a bit." He was the youngest man of the lot, and yet they obeyed him. Some men are born to command —they do it unconsciously, and with no thought of assuming any kind of dictatorship. Others obey, and without resentment, glad of a will that is stronger than theirs, and of a purpose that does not waver. In a few minutes Justin found himself quite alone. He saw the others vanishing like spectres in the dim moonlight. For a few minutes he sat on the bank, and rested his face in his hands. How strangely this unseen girl was dominating his thoughts—his very life How completely she absorbed all his interests. Suddenly he rose to his feet, and put his hand to his mouth, as though to shout to his companions; then he thought better of it. "One is as good as ten," he said to himself. "I'll go alone. Dorothy says she is passionately fond of the sea." And he turned and strode rapidly up the hillside. In a few minutes he was standing on the cliff looking down at the incoming tide, that raced and rippled in the moonlight with a sound that was full of music. The long strip of beach that was still uncovered lay in deep shadow. It would not be high water for more than an hour yet. After standing for awhile looking and listening, he commenced to descend the very path that Ruby had descended earlier in the day. He threaded his way with caution, for he knew what one false step might mean. Down, and still down, the easy zig-zag, then he paused suddenly, and put his hand to his ear and listened.

. . . . . . . . Meanwhile a larger wave than usual had splashed its cold salt water all over Ruby's face. It seemed a cruel, hungry wave, but in reality it was kind; it awoke her from a long trance in which she had lain, and restored her once more to consciousness. With a little cry, she sat up, and stared wonder- ingly around her. For several minutes she was unable to realise who she was, or where she was, or what had happened, and all the while the waves kept crawling in and in, soaking her pretty white dress, and chilling her almost to the bone. At length, as in a sudden flash, everything came back to her, and with another cry she tried to struggle to her feet; but she dropped back again with a bitter moan of pain; she was too hurt to move without the most excruciating torture, and yet to sit still would simply be to drown by slow inches. Every moment the tide came in further and further. How cruel it seemed to her now, as it sucked away the sand on which she sat, and mocked her in her misery. She was too terror-stricken to lose consciousness again. Every faculty of the mind was preter- naturally active. She thought of the mental agony of her mother, of the distress of the Pentyres, of the sorrow of her father when he returned from London. "Oh, I cannot die here like this by slow inches I' she said to herself. "Oh, it, is horrible" horrible," and she screamed aloud for help in her terror. It was this cry that arrested Justin's attention. As he came cautiously down the cliff-path the next moment the cry was repeated. And with a shout: "I'm coming!" distance at a break-neck pace, flinging caution to the winds, and coming within an ace of destruction more than once. In an incredibly short time he was at the &go "f the cliffs, looking eagerly right and left; then another faint cry for help drew his eyes in the right direction. A long way out beyond the edge of the tide, it seemed to him, the head and shoulders of someone rose dimly above the water. With another shout: "I'm coming! "he splashed into the creeping waves, and in a moment he was by her side. "You are Ruby Loveday ?" he questioned, stoop. ing down and gathering her up in his strong arms. "Yes, we are staying at Endilloe, Do you think you can save me ? ' "I can try, at any rate," he said, with a laugh, uAre you much hurt ? " "I'm afraid lam a good deal. I was unable to move from where I was. "It's lucky I found you," he said, making a strong effort to keep his voice steady. "We've been searching for you for hours, and had almost given you up as lost." He had begun to ascend the cliff-path by this time, and he soon found, strong as he was, that he was not carrying a child. "I must ask you to clasp your arms about my neck, if you have strength to do so," he said, a curious thrill running through him as he did so. She answered him by doing as he told her. The situation was not without its touch of romance. Her head, with its wealth of dripping hair, rested on his shoulder, her cheek was close to his. He could feel her breath upon his neck, as he climbed higher and higher; and it seemed to put new strength into him. Now and then a groan of pain escaped her lips, and he felt instinctively how resolutely she was trying to keep still. He made no attempt to look at her. He could not if he tried. Her face was too close to his. Occasionally, when the pain was not so severe, she could not help wondering who he was. She had caught more than one glimpse of his face in the moonlight, and he seemed to her to be a very handsome man. And then how strong he was I She seemed but a mere child in his arms! Half-way up the cliff he sat down on a ledge of sock and rested himself. She did not remove her bead from his shoulder nor her arms from about Ibis neck. "I fear you are in great pain," he said, with a sing of genuine sympathy in his voice. "I am at times," she answered. "I'm afraid my left' foot is smashed all to pieces." "I hope it is not so bad as that," he said; "but we shall soon be home now." "Do you live near here?" she questioned. "At Endilloe. lam Justin Pentyre." "Oh, I am so glad. Dorothy has often spoken of you. But you are nothing like I pictured you." No?" . _

She did not reply until they got to the top of the cliff. Then she said: "How strong you are Oh, I shall never be able to thank you enough as long as I live.*

"Don't try," he said. "I don't want any thanks ; to have saved you is reward enough." And he hurried across the strip of common, and was soon in the fir plantation. Here he had tw thread his way with care, on account of Ruby's damaged foot; but, thanks to the great round moon that was now shining in all her splendour, he found no difficulty. He was half disposed to raise a shout, when he got out on the Endilloe side; but, on second thoughts, he decided to carry Ruby all the way, and keep all the glory of it to himself. "How distressed mother will be to see me in this plight!" Ruby said, as they descended the field towards the orchard. _ .

"She will forget everything in her gladness to see you alive." "Yes, so she will," and then he felt scalding tears running down his neck. For a moment these tears quite unmanned him. He felt weak and nerveless. He wanted to sit down and rest. Then he was seized with a pas- sionate desire to raise her face and kiss away her tears, and comfort her as her mother might. He quickly recovered himself, however. He remembered her position and his own. She was the only daughter of a wealthy London merchant, and he was a salesman in a village store. "I'd better not spoil everything by making a fool of myself at the beginning," he reflected, and he pushed open the orchard gate with his foot. By this time every nerve in his body was tinglan with excitement. The lost was found, and he alon. know of it. He was the bringer of good tidings He could not contain himself any longer, and he raised a great shout: "Ahoy! Ahoy! Ahoy!" In a moment three women came rushing out of the house. Dorothy and the two mothers. "Is that you, Justin ? " Dorothy cried. "Aye. I've found her," he shouted. "She's here alive. Ahoy ! Ahoy!" And the shout was taken up in the distant fields. Ahoy! Ahoy And the night wind carried the cry into Tregarrick Wood, and the searchers turned right-about-face, and responded with the same thrilling cry: " Ahoy ! Ahoy!" for they knew what it meant; and in a few moments the thud of running feet was borne in from the distant fields, and middle-aged men leaped hedges with an agility they had not shewn since they were young. In the meanwhile Mrs. Loveday had cried for a few moments on Ruby's neck; then she led the way into the house. Justin still bore his burden bravely, though he was all but spent. Through the big kitchen he followed the women, along the passage, and into the Lovedays' room. Ruby Was in a state of collapse when he laid her down on a big sofa, and for several minutes she appeared to be in a dead faint. Justin sank back into an easy-chair, and gripped the elbows with his hands, for a thin mist had come up before his eyes, and the room seemed to be spinning round like a whirligig at a fair. No one, however, took any notice of him. Every eye in the room but his was fixed on Ruby. She looked a strangely bedraggled nymph as she lay there on the sofa. Her hair was tangled and dripping. Her dress was soaked with the sea- water, and clung tightly to her figure. Sea-sand was all over hor, on her eyebrows and in her ears. Her lips were tightly shut, her face was as white as the dead.

Dorothy stood by chafing her cold hands, and Mrs. Loveday was doing her best to get some brandy between her teeth. In the meanwhile the hum of voices and the clatter of hob-nailed shoes were heard in the big kitchen. A dozen voices appeared to be asking the same question at the same time, and Mrs. Pentyre was doing her best to explain matters. Justin, in his easy-chair, heard like one who is only half-awake. He had no desire to rouse him- self, or to take any further part. The reaction had set in. He was more completely spent than ever he had been before in his life. He heard his father give orders to Nathan to ride across to St. lago, Churchtown, and fetch Dr. Morrison at once; and he tried to rouse himself to say he would go. But the effort was too great. Then he heard Mrs. Loveday say, with a thrill of joy in her voice: "She is cominr, round at last. We must get her upstairs and into bed as quickly as possible." "Justin will carry her upstairs," Dorothy said, quickly, and she turned at once and looked at her brother.

The next moment she was kneeling by his side. "Are you not well, Justin? " she said, anxiously. "Oh, yes, I'm all right," he said, trying to smile ; "just a little spent, that is all. Fetch me a glass of water, will you, and I shall be all right." "Drink this," said Mrs. Loveday, coming for- ward, and Justin drank it, not knowing what it was, and in a few minutes the mist cleared away from his eyes, and the spinning room slowed down, and at length stood still. "Now I shall see her face," was his thought. "I wonder if she is anything like I pictured her?"

CHAPTER VIII.

MAR'S EXTREMITY. We do our best, and wait : The issue rests with God."

Ruby's dishevelled condition seemed rathei to add to her charm than detract from it. She was one of those dainty creatures that look pretty under any circumstances. Her face was such a perfect oval, her features so delicrady cut, her complexion so clear, her eyes so larq,c and expressive, and her smile so sweet and radiant, that if she were only a flower-seller in the street people would be sure to turn and look at her a second time. Justin rose from his chair and looked at her with an expression of wonder in his eyes. The light of the lamp fell full upon her face, and lit up her large expressive eyes. She was not a bit like the Ruby of his imagination. The tall, fair, languid beauty, with golden hair and pink and white complexion, might exist somewhere, but her name was not Ruby Loreday. This brown-haired maiden was of quite another type. She greeted him with one of her sweetest smiles. "I am sorry to have to trouble you again," she said; "but would you mind carrying me upstairs to my room ?—you are so strong. He had felt weak enough a moment before; but such an appeal would give a giant's strength to a much feebler man than he. He saw that she was fighting pain and weakness with desperate energy. She wore a smile upon her face when it would have been a relief to her to have shrieked. "I fear it will give you great pain to move you," he said, sympathetically. "I know it will," she said, with the same sweet smile playing about her lips; "but it has to be borne, and I must make the best of it." He did not speak again, but bent over her at once. She stretched out her hands and clasped him about the neck as she had done before. Very gently and tenderly he gathered her up in his arms. All his strength came back to him with a rush. She seemed but as a child. Dorothy went before with a light, and Mrs. Loveday followed. Poor Ruby cried out more than once as Justin slowly climbed the stairs ; but he reached the top at length, and went forwari into Ruby's room. Without a word, he laid her on the low bed, and turned away. There was a singing in his ears again, and his heart was beating at fever speed. Down in the big kitchen a dozen men were wait- ire" for him, eager to hear the story he had to tell. But he was in no humour for conversation. He wanted to be alone, so that he might commune with his own heart. 'Nevertheless he did his best to satisfy the natural curiosityse:f his neighbours. "But why did 'cc send us oal warm way while you went the t'other ? " asked a keen-eyed little man, William Sparrow, by name. "I don't know," Justin answered, indifferently. "It was just a sudden impulse, that is all." "And do you think she is much hurt? " his father asked. "I am afraid she is." was the answer. "Yon see, she lay for hours quite unconscious, and that means more than a hurt foot." "She seems a terrible plucky little maid," said an old farmer, after emptying his mouth of a cloud of tobacco smoke. "You are right," said Justin, with sudden energy; "she is plucky. But here comes the doctor, I fancy. We shall know soon the extent of her injuries." For the next half-hour the old eight-day clock could be 'heard very distinctly in the room. All attempts at conversation ended in a failure. John Pentyre fetched out his tobacco jar, and a handful of slay pipes for those who smoked; the others sat round ia all manner of attitudes and stared at nothing in particular. Every now and then some.one turned his head and looked at the clock: the minutes travelled by slowly. Mrs. Pentyre came into the room at length, and went to the stove to get some hot water. She looked very grave, and her eyes were wet as though she had been crying. "Well, mother," said John Pentyre, " how is the little maid going on ? " "I hardly know," was the hesitating reply. "I'm afraid she's a great deal more hurt than any of us think." "Does Dr. Morrison say so?"

"He says nothing, but he looks very grave, and he hinted just now that her foot was not the worst. part of her injuries." Justin looked up eagerly, but he did not ask any luestions ; and Mrs. Pentyre immediately after left the room, closing the door behind her. Then the tick of the old clock filled the room vain, for no one was in the humour to dispute its right to be heard. At length, after what seemed an unconscionably long time, the doctor's heavy footsteps sounded on the stairs, and John and Justin rose at once, and went out into the hall to intercept him. They came back again five minutes later looking very grave. The others stood up, and waited for the verdict. . . . . .

Justin replied at once to their inquiring looks. "The doctor declines to give any very definite opinion at present. He admits that her injuries are serious, but how serious he cannot tell yet. He will be better able to judge to-morrow morning." The little company melted rapidly away after- that. Some said good-night, or what was intended for "good-night," for, generally speaking, the word "good" was treated as being entirely super- fluous; others stole out into the summer moonlight without speaking. John filled his pipe again when his neighbours had left him, but Justin stole wearily and dejectedly off to bed. For a long time he lay with strained attention, likening for every sound. Once or twice he fancied he heard a low moan of pain, and the sound shot through him like a knife. He wondered why it was that he was so troubled and anxious. Had it been Dorothy herself who lay ill, he could not have been more concerned. This Ruby Loveday was nothing to him— But, stop. The events of that evening had changed everything. He had saved her life, and by that act he had created a right— But what right? Well, the right to be interested in her. She could never be again as a stranger to him. That which you save is, in a measure, yours. He had established a claim which, though he would never insist upon he could never forget its existence. He fell asleep at length, and for seven hours slept the sleep of utter weariness and exhaustion, and when he woke next morning to the chirruping of innumerable birds he felt little if any the worse for the experiences of the previous night. Dorothy did not put in an appearance at break- fast. She had been up all night, and was now taking a little rest. Mrs. Pentyre could say little that was favourable respecting the patient. In fact, she seemed in great pain and considerably more feverish than on the previous night. Justin went off to rfrelford with a heavy heart. He was beginning to be afraid that he had only saved Ruby from death in one form that she might meet it in another. When he got back in the evening he found that Mr. Loveday had just returned from London, and that Ruby had brightened up considerably directly he came into the room, and that she seemed much better than earlier in the day. This was satisfactory news, and Justin ate his supper with a much better appetite than he had shewn at breakfast. •I But the days that followed were days of great anxiety and of gradually lessening hope. Each morning the news from the sick room seemed less promising than on the previous morning. The patient was bright and cheerful, and talked of getting better directly; but those who kept watch by her saw that she got gradually weaker, and that the hope of recovery was vanishing farther and, farther into the distance. Justin was consumed with a longing he could not understand to see her; yet no one ever suggested such a thing, and he had not the courage to ask. He envied Dorothy the privilege of being so con- stantly by her side, and was too proud to admit by word or look that his anxiety was becoming almost too painful to be borne. Mr. Loveday talked no more about business. It was mainly for his child that he toiled from year to year, and added to his already ample fortune. But now that she seemed slipping from his grasp, business was nothing. All his life seemed wrapped up in Ruby. Each morning and afternoon lie went out for a short walk, the rest of his time he spent in the sick room. He did not say much, but he watched every change in the invalid with an eagerness that was almost painful. On the fourth day a specialist was called in from Plymouth, and on the sixth day the Lovedays' family doctor journeyed down from London, but to, the onlookers it seemed as if nothing could save her. 'The doctors said very little, and that little was anything but of a hopeful character. Mr. Loveday was very reticent, but it "was. evident that he was preparing for the worst. On the tenth day the whisper ran through the house, and found its way out into_ the fields and was carried across the valley to St. lago, Churchtown, that "the doctors could de no more for the little maid from London; that the issue now was in the hands of Providence." It was early closing day, and Justin had hurried home with the speed of the wind. Dorothy met him at the door and told him what the doctors had said. For a monient he looked at her as if about to ask a question, then he turned awaf and wandered down across the al.'s. It was a beautiful sunshiny day, with a cool, pleasant wind blowing in from the sea. All around him the cornfields were yellowing in the summer sun, and the air was steeped with the odours of the fields and pine wood. But for Justin there was no sunshine and no fragrance. Had anyone predicted three weeks before what tie was now passing through he would have laughed at the suggestion. He had made up his mind to dislike the Lovedays and all their ways ; yet now he felt he would willingly die himself if by that means he could save Ruby's life. In one of the fields he came upon Amos Blue. "An' 'ow be the little maid ? Amos asked, stretching his back and looking inquiringly at Justin. "Very ill, Amos, very ill. The doctors can do no more." "Is that so, now ?," Amos said, dropping his eyes. "Is that so? Then what is to be done, Mr. Justin ? " "Nothing can be done, Amos," Justin answered, a little bitterly, "except leave her in the hands of Providence." "An' be you goin' to do that ? " Amos questioned, with the faintest suggestion of a smile upon his weather-beaten face. "Be the doctors goin' to leave her there ? " "Well, I suppose so. There is nothing else they can do." "Ah, well, then, there may be a chance for the little maid now," and Amos smiled quite broadly. "What do you mean, Amos ? " "I main, Mr. Justin, that man's extremity _es M God's oppportunity. Have 'ee forgot that, r. Justin? 'You've oal been trustin' in the doctors afore, now you be agoin to trust in the Lord. An' I do say that there's a chance now for the little maid. When men fail then the Almighty comes in," and Amos turned to his work again. Justin looked up and saw Dorothy hurrying across the field towards him. (To be coatinued.) •

Warrington Co-operative Society. The annual meeting of the Warrington Co-opera- tive Society task place on Monday evening, Mr T Birchen presiding. In his opening remarks he re- minded the meeting that this was not only the first meeting of the society this century bur, it was the 40th anniversary of its existence. Forty years ago a few working men, of whom some ot the present directors formed a part, met, and subscribed about .2100 capital, with which they commenced business in a small shop in Cairo-qtreet,rented at £lB a year, the sales being come £3O to £4O per week. The present capital was £54,000, and the sales over £2,000 per week.ln addition to this the society were the owners of even of the best block 4 of freehold prop trty in the locality. It was paying in dividends and interest over £16,000 per annum to its members, and its sales last year exceeded those of the previous year by £9,100. (Applause.; Looking at the Co-opera- tive movement generally, the results were astound- ing. Taking the 36 years from 1862 to 1897,be found that in the first year the sales in the United King- dom were .£2 333,500, but in 1897 they reached £64,956,000. he total profits paid curing the 36 years amounted to £84,601,452. The Co-operative Wholesale Society's sales alone were ROW over four- teen millions per annum. As 1).-.; had often :told them. there was no reform for the bemlt of the people that they had net the power to effect if they ; bad faith in their principles. In Vie words of Lord Rosehery, addressing ' a Cc-operative Con " What I admire is that you do not go to ruin to do sorrethieg tor you, you are self-helpers, do it for ynareolves." (Applause.) The rat then proceeded to pass ins balance-sheet, showed a dividend of 31 in the pound wii usual depteciation on the various properties society. To Cures in a Lancashire Family.

Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Fletcher have no fewer than eleven olive branches, all residing with them at No. 51, Keatsley-street, Daisy Bill, Westhoughton, on the outskirts of Bolton. To this address a reporter on the Bolton Chronicle hied the other day with along list of enquiries to be addressed to the head of the house. He was most cordially received, and after the briefest of introduc- Slone was placed in possession of the following singular a tsry by Mrs and Miss Fletcher. who will vouch for every detail. John Fetcher is 24 years of age and the oldest of eleven children. He had suffered terribly from tosthaehe, and finally had the tooth extracted ; but the remedy proved far worse than the disease, for he became the.victins of partial facial paralysis. One eye utterly refused to remain closod, his month was drawn, and general health was poor. Naturally the whole family, were much concerned and medical aid was sought. Medicine was administered regularly, but failed to produce the slightest effect. " We accordingly decided " said Mrs. Fletcher, "to try Dr. Williams Pink Pills for Pale People, of which we had some keewledge. Our Eliza had been under the doctor for anaemia, and he said she was just bordering on consumption. She seemsd to make no progress whatever, so at last! bought a supply of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, being encouraged to try them by reading in the paper cases of antemia and threatened c onsumption cured by them. The result was • speedy and perfect cure. One box gave her strength and restored her failing appaite, and she has not ailed anything since. When my son was taken ill in such an alarming manner we accoriingly com- menced a course of the pills, and for the second time we had a complete cure. There is now not the slightest trace of facial weakness. My son is as Malt as eVerlhe was, his appetite is good and he iS able to do a hard day's work in the position of dataller without:the slight- est ill effects. All the nsighbours know of his cure and think ita wonder- fal one. It was done in less time than was spent in taking doc- tor's medicine. The fret box made him feel better, We second set the paralysed part twitching, and after the third box had been con- sumed he declared himself to be all right, and time has happily proved that there was no mistake bout the matter, for be is now cheerful and as well in health as ever be was in his life.

She was brought very low indeed, and the doctor who had been attending her stated after the third week that he was afraid rapid consumption had set in, and asked to be allowed to cael in another medical man tor consultation. WITH THE VOLUNTEERS TO STAN- DERTON AND BACK.

Interesting Accouut.by a Warrington

Volunteer.

Private R Irons has writnn an accouat of his experienc is with the First Sot Lancashire Ser- vice Company. He yam one 0 the Volunteers who left Warrington in February of last year with Captain Appleton, but who was invalided 11311:1iiii September last. The Voyage to the Cape. -

After referring to-the stay of the Volunteers in Orford Barracks prior to their departure, Private Irons proceeds to state that it was on the 23rd of February, the Volunteers left tan thrraelte tJ make the best of their way to Bank Quay station. To march in order was an utter impos.ibilty. The townspeople not only took possession of the streets but the Volunteers also. We seeme4, h. says, to be public property for many of the more demonstra- tive !wiles along the line of route forcibly took us in their fond embrace. It was •wad great difficulty we reached the platform of the statku in ones and twos after a terrible crushing. We took it all in good humour, but the seene will never be forgotten by me. The train left Bank Qaay station for Sawhimpton at 1.30 a.m. arriving at SAtthamptou at 8 a.m. We then gat an hoard the es. Avondale Castle, and stored our kits aril :inns away, everythiog on board being nicely laid out, as we were not travelling as troopers, but as 2nd class passengers. [he food Was very good. We set sail at 3 pm. amid ringing cheers. Everybody WB9 on deck but before night there were very few who could say tney tutilu'c been sick. Tnere were sharks, porpoia-is, flying fish and gulls about the boat occasionally. Whales were very scarce, only two being seen all the way to Table Bay. We reached Las' Palmas, or Grand Canary, as it is calm]. no the 29;11 of February. This Was our first stopping place. We did not land, but could seo from wh.re we were anchored that it was a very nice place. Smill boats CAIII9 around ours with Spaniards on them selling oranges, bananas, tobacco and comical. We took in coal as the steamer consumed such a large quantity, averaging about 50 tons per thy. We lett on the 1s: of March for Cape- town. We had parades, sports and c incerta daily on board to pass the time away, as it would have been very wearying without something to do. It was a treat to see , a passing ship, and beautiful to see the sun setting over the horiz sn. On our wav to Capetown we had signalled !to us that Cronje was taken prisoner, and 4,000 of his men. We signalled it to the other boats and they signalled hack that Ladysmith was relieved. We arrived at Table Bay, Capeto mu, at 12 p.m on March 16;n, and anchored, going into the dock at 12 a.m. on March 17, the distance fv+rn Smthimptou being 5958 miles.

First Experiences in South Africa. We dLembarked and marched to Green Point Camp. We had a hard time pitching tents as the ground was so hard. Next day (Sunday) we were on half rations, dry bread and tea. At night I had a walk round Capetown, and it is an attractive place. It was a treat to see the ladies dressed in all the bright cilours they could get hold of, and being principally black, they looked very well. Oa Monday, the 19th, we had a route march, going about two miles just to put us in trim again. The guns that were tsk9n from Cronje were there. We left Capetown on the ss. Guelph, on Marcia 20th. She was a very dirty ship. We called at Port Enzabeth on March 22nd te unload cargo. A great many Kaffirs were employed, and they said, "Me no like Dutchman ; Englishman my friend." We left here and arrivad at East London and unloaded cargo. The York and 14inca8:er Volunte,-re got off here. We left on 25th March and arrived at Durban on the 27th, hut were in quarantine until 28th and then disem- barked. We put our kits away in the railway carriages, and were allowed to have a look round Durb‘n for 3 how's. Here we saw rickshaws with Zulus pulling theni, these vehicles being used in place of cabs. Joined the "Old Fortieth."

We left by the train, passinz Co:enso and Ladysmith and on March 29th joined the Regiment (the Old Fight- ing Fortieth) at Surprise Hill Camp. We were just in time to have a hetrty breakfast as a welcome from the Battalion. We had drill in the afternoon, and next moraine we had a hathing, parade in the Klipp River. In the afternoon I saw the wreck of the Long Torn that was blown over at Ladysmitb. We marched from here a distance of about 8 miles, which took U 3 from 84. m. until 1 p.m., and then bivouacked at Modder Spruit. We had to cook our own dinners and then go On outpost duty until 6 a.m. next morning. We then went escort to two naval runs at night. We were working making bangers until 9 a.m. next merming, as we expected an attack. At break of day we marched about 5 miles, arriving at Elandslasgte. where the battle of Eispdh. lasgte was fought. We had plenty of outpost duty sangar making and road making. Our hands were cover.d with blisters with using the pick and spade. On May 10th we had target practic,, at Elands,aagte. The grass was very bag rising, from 3 to 8 feet. A Long March.

We left here, which is called Janonas Kop, on May 14tri at 6 p.m. Leaving our outpoat we went to Grog- mans Farm about 10 miles distance, arriving at 10 o'clock. We then went to finish our outpost which was until 6 a.m. oa May 15th. We tren marched on Wessels Nek without opposition, the distance being 15 mils, and it took us from 5.30 a.m. untd 1.30 p.m. A tremen- dous number fell out on this mßrch, as it was so hot and fatiguing. On May 16:h we marched on to Moran, miles away, where we wont no outpost, and those who were not on sentry went to a Kaffir kraal looting, and found same hens, oranges, and lemons. It was a tea parry we were having, On Mar 17th we came off outpost and escorted naval guns to Washbank Farm. On the following cley vr. marched to Glenco-• Junction, arriving at 8 a.m. and off again at 2 p.m toHatmicaFar,n6 miles away. We had to march over burnt grass and we also had a sand storm. All of us were as black as niggers but were able to 4et a wish Kg Liero was a river close by and is was a luxury. On Ma g 18.11 we marched to Dannhaustr 12 miles away. After staying a =holt time for orders we got one. Fell Out on the Way.

I fell uur. on the way and load to get into a hospital waggon. We rested at night and biv .uacsd as it was too much for one march. This was the first time of my getting into a tent for 6 weeks. After bc-ing examined by the doctor, I was found to be suffering from enteric fever. Next morning I had to get my kit into the ambulance watsgon and follow unto Standerton,reaehing there an June 17th I was in hospital days being In a bell tent that held two. I was moved about half a dozen times into different tents, seeing men dying and others delirous with fever. As soon as they died they were re- moved out of the tent aid buried. Shortly afterwards I came down country from Standerton in tho hospital train, Princess Christian to Newcastle. It ii a beautiful train everythin4 b-ing nJe fiCed uo. - -In the Hospital. . . . . - " " •

I was carried from the train on a stretcher to the hospital. The tent had eight of us in bat the other seven were on rations, but I wasn't allowed aaythirig but milk, soda water and limejuice which was poor food for a good appetite. I stayed here a mantn sad when they brought nae my kit, I fouud the following articles missing :—Field glasses, purse, packet book, Swan Fountain pen, boots, mess-tin, water bottle, field service cap, Kersey coat, and an Absent Minded Wggac shirt. I came from here to Howiek in Natal through Coleus() and Ladysmith on the way. it took from 2 p.m. June , 23rd uotil 8 a.m. 24th. It took so long because it was ordinary train fitted up as an hospital train and they changed engines at Frere and the guard went to sleep on the platform and the driver was missing so we were I delayed a w bile. The hospital at It °wick is built of corrugated iron and there are also teats for the convalescent. I was wall nursed by an Australian lady who was very different to some of them. From June 15th to August 9th I never had any solid food so you may know what a luxury it was for me to be allowed bread again. On August 19h I was allowed up for two hours per day until August 22nd when I was allowed up for four hours. On August 26:h I had a relapse with pneumonia and was in awful pain for seven days when I was all right again. One of our doctors went sick with pneumonia on the Tuesday and was buried on the Thursday. Private Fred Woods died of en- teric on September 16th and was buried at Howick Cemetery September 17th. I came from Howick at 8 a.m., arriving at Durban at 4.30 p.m., stopping half au hour at Pietermaritzburg for dinner. We Rot on board the Hospital ship Dunera for home and arrivrd at Southampton on October 18th On returning to Orford Barr aoks,to wait for furlough or discharge I took the latter so as to get owe more in civilian attire which I did on October 23rd. If anyone wants to know any more about soldiering let them go and enlist or else inside a barrack room,which I dare- say would be sufficicent. for a good many. A soldier's life may be all well in time and peace and some may think there is a glory in being able to sport about in a red tunic, but if yon want to know what it is to be a soldier in real earnest, go on active service.

NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. [By 7-&-ialist.l

Warrington failed to beat Miilom after all, in fact the performance of the locals was truly miserable. They ought to have won easily, but their play was so Eo.ock- ingly bad that the Millom men won the match by a solitary try to a penalty goal. TO score ought to have bee, really, something like tw-Ive points to nil in faviur:of the visitors, judging by the:ruo oF the play and the chances which presented themselves. Warrington's weakest point is in taking chances whic c rtiis their way,and many a match has been lost by a pore inability to score under the easiest conditions. There is an immmse contrast between Runcorn and Warrington in this respo,:t, Co,. the former are ose of the smartest clubs in th,, League it snapping up the smallest chances of getting points. And they generally get them too.

.1 worse day for foo'ball than Saturday could hardly have been imagined, fn. rain fell unceasingly and reduced the ground to nothing better than a quagmire. Correct football WAS an impossibility and this much was in favour of toe home team whose strong point is not skill. The weather and the ground put the teams pretty much on a level, but Warrinetln were the superior lot far as attacking was concerned. How they failed to score on several occasions was quite remarkable. Never- theless if either team deserved to win it was Millom for they tried hard and played as well as could he expected while Warrington gave a display which absolutely dis- graced them.

Both sides suffered from absentees, Warrington having to do without Isherwood and Harris, whose presence would have made considerable difference. Fish turned out and, in the opinion of most, lost the game for his club. This is unusual Lir him, bet he played centre and was so sure that no one could score but himself that he never gave the other backs a chance. Being a celebrity he was well watched by the Millorn men so that his efforts were fruitless as well as selfish. A timely pass would en more than one occasion have resulted in Elockenhall or Ratcliffe getting over. Things seemed doomed to go wrong, however, and Dickenson also played a very poor game. Burgess and Bate played well at half, which tempts one to remark that the three- quarters and halves never —or hardly ever—seem to be in form on the same day. Hallam played his usual hard, reliable game, and the forwards stulk up well consider- ing their lack of weight. Ratcliffe played smartly, and was on the day's play, the best of the three-quarters.

Millom have to come to Wiiderspool yet, like Barrow the apdointed visitors this week-end. So that Warring- ton should have the opportunity of tasting sweet re- venge. They ought to pulver;sl the Barrowians, but it is quite certain that the latter will not enbrait meekly to any such treatment.

The great and over-whelming surprise of Saturday was toe defeat of Swinton at St Helens, by two tries to one. There had been hints that the Saints might repeat last year's performance but no one believed such a thing possible. Yet the unexpected happened and Swinton's unbeaten record is spoiled. St Helens fully rec-gniaed that the weather was in their favour and making the game a forward one they smothered the lordly lions in a style the latter failed to relish. So badly did the "Beecham" go dawn in fact, that Swinton have pro- tested again, presumably because the St Helene ground was not a grass-plot sad the home players did not wear kid gloves. lam more ashamed of Swinton over such unsportstnanlilo conduct than I was of Old- ham last week. Moreover I'll lay a new hat to a hair- pi3 that the ground was a lot better than that at Roch- dale when Warrington were kaocked out of the cup- competition last winter.

Other clubs performed as expected, Runcorn winning a hard game at Leigh by a try to nil, The " Linnets " are a 800 1,, and this must rank ss one of their best periermancei during tho present season.

I referred last week to the race between the points for and against of the Warrington cub. and speculated as to which would gain supremacy. Saturday's match bas not helped me much, for the contestants have run a dead heat and stand at " 104 all." There are 18 goals, 24 tries each way, and the figures are more symmetrical than creditable. Only eight matches have losen won out of nineteen played. Last season 12 ow: of 26 were won, so that that reo,rd ought to bo equalled as War- rington play Barrow, Broughton Rangers, Stockport, Millom an L.?gh at how vot Fier, Ilea he only Com- fort, unless we do well in the Cup Competition. --- The Lea4uoi:table up to date is as follows :

WAS IT A LOAN OR A GIFT?

Claim against a Warrington Butcher.

At the r Warrington County Court on Thursday last week before its lion air, Judge Bowen Row- lands, Q.C., Ellen Alice Cox, who resides in Man- chester-road, claimed the sam of .£l5 from Thomas , Withers, a butcher, carrying on business in Orford- lane—Mr Steel appeared for the plaintiff and defendant was represented byMr Browne. The facts of the case, as stwed by the plaintiff's solicitor, were to the effect that the -plaintiff was the widow of the late Mr William Cox, a pork butcher, who carried on business at 14, Winwick- street. The defendant had been in his employ for a number of years. Mr Cox died in 1897 and Mrs Cox carried on the business until the end of January 1893, retaining the defendant's services. After giving up the busineis, Mrs Cox was remov- ing to a private house in Manchester-road and was disposing of certain articles of furniture.A. portion of it was taken to a Miss Bancroft by the defendant and in conversation with her be said he wag going to start business and was in want of about .£lO At his request she promised to ask Mrs Cox to lend him the money, The following morning the plaintiff had a conversation with the defeAdant and it was arranged that he should call at her iJouse in Manchester-road for the money. He called about February 14th and Mrs Cox told him that she thought .£lO was rather little to commence'busiae-s with and offered to lend him £l5. wnich the defendant accepted. In August 1899 Mrs Cox saw the defendant and asked him if he was going to refund the m mey. He replied that he could not pay her just then as he was being pressed by other creditors but he would do so as soon as he possibly could. Some time afterwards she made out an account showing £l5 due to her and sent it to the defendant by Mr. Bamford, her brother-in-law. Defendant told him the same story 'Nirs Cox again saw the defendant in Winwick. street on the Ist of November last and asked him whether iie could not see his way clear to pay the money. lie said" No you must wait a minute. She asked him w:iat he meant oy saying that, and he said" What do 7 owe you?" She told him that he knew very well it was £l5, and he said "1 thought you gave ms that:' On her telling him that she did nut give the money to him be said "You wilt Cave to wait "and went away. On being secs again he said he did not owe the money, and she had no witnesses to prove that he did.

Plaintiff gave evidance bearing out this state- ment. Crose-axamined by Mr Browne ebe denied having promised to give the defendant a present if he would remain in her employ. Evidence was also gives by Mary Bancroft and Joseph Bamford.

Foi• the defence Mr Browne said that the defend- ant bad worked faithfully tor tie late Mr Cox for 14 years, and on the death of Mr Cox, the plain- tiff tilred him to manage the business until she diem posed of it and she promised to give him a handsome present. He also pointed out that there was no statement in writin" in reference to the debt. Defendanr, in the course of lila evidence stated that Mrs Cox tnlel n.m ',net instead of lending him £lO she would give him ..£l5 and hoped it would do him good.

~ His lionour gave judgment for the plaintiff with cons.

Warrington Field Club. The opening meeting ot the second half of the winter session was hell in the Museum Lecturs Room, when Mr. Charles White, the president, lectured on "Volcanic Sills in Skye" For several years past the doctor has denoted his summer holi- days to a careful study of the geology of Skye,witk special reference to the evidences of volcanic action which that island, in common wiih other places along the west of Scotland and a large area of the north of Ireland, presents. PC ith the aid of diagrams, and of a series of btautiful and effective lantern slides, the lecturer laid before his audience a sketce of the probable order in which the succes- sive outbursts of subterranean molten matter had occurred, the general nature of t:.e ejected matter, cod the present appearance of ‘the remains, after being worn down by ins:. and rain for some thou- sands of centuries. That, the tendency to equalisa- tion of temperature has not et resulted in the dis- appearance of local irregularities is proved by the tact that in borings in Antrim the thermometer rises with increasing th.pth at twice the ascertained rate of Lancashire b.)riilg,. Jhe lecturer aid not suggest that there was any grave risk of either Antrim fissures or the Skye craters, which in Tertiary times devastated large tracts of country, again becoming active; so trat members might take their summer holidays in either region with- out anxiety.

ITHE whole world] The World's Tribute. mourns with Englandl and in her days of darkness!

sorrow. Whether the rulers be absolute, ori ititutional, or Republican, they are sending usl their sympathy, and their peoples feel that a great (figure in the world is laid low. Their own holdi Ton the past seems to be loosened. QUEEN "wronrA stood for them a symbol of stabilityl -Aidst change. Russia has had several Czars (Germany three Emperors, France several forms of warm:tient, Austria and America many vicissi- tudes, Italy a resuscitation, during the late QUEEN'S reign. Whilst the face of the world to be changing England remained un-

Juched under the rule of a benignant Sovereign ind her general policy has been one of peace, /order, and progress. It is impossible the nations i of the world can behold unmoved the event which happened on Tuesday, because it means so much in the way of instruction and' retrospect, inspiration and prophecy. To endurel, 'there must be healthy response to popular feeling, hobriety in the Monarch, the Emperor or the Pre- sident, and continuity of idea and feeling in the I (people themselves. This is the lesson of Englandi Ito the world, and it is being appreciated andl Istudied. The lesson of the world to England isl that high character, sobriety of purpose, and good Tenius will always command admiration and irship. To-day the world bears its glad testi- mony to the greatness of a dead Sovereign. The personal wealth and the successful rule of QUEEN IVICTORIA are admitted from one hemisphere to 1 Ithe other, and wherever the name of England is -lowa there are tears for the dead and sympathy tor the livir.g. Never before in modern times has there been such a splendid recognition of the 1 greatness of a Sovereign and the power of the people over whom she has ruled. When the great monarchs and , Emperors of the past were lying dead in their chambers half the world knew noth- ing

and cared nothing about their fate. Therel was no tribute of affection, there was no hymn of, praise over a magnificent career. The modern world is more united. Electricity has been the union of nations. Admiration for a high, Lcharacter makes the Whole world kin. The!

of a great country is .that its posi

_ is recognised when its ruler falls and- its day of tribulation begins. The world is bear ling its many-voiced tribute to our dead Sovereign las well as to a living people. If we have enemies lthey are compelled by respect for truth and the magic of a noble personality to recognise that ourl strength is colossal, unbroken,and likely to remain.l Our hearts are smitten, our famous Ruler has'

/passed away, but every eulogy on the dead is a Irecognition of the virtue of the.lliving, and Great FBritain remains what it was, even though its Icentral character has disappeared from the stage. It is especially gratifying to note with what 'unanimity the Monarchs, principalities, and 'powers of the world lay their tributes at the footi of QUEEN VICTORIA'S bier,and how sad the nation' aro that a good lady, and a noble mother, and iwise ruler has left them, poorer, as if a bright star/

4bad been quenched in the dark blue sky. We are grateful as well as proud. There has been talk of ; splendid isolation, but it has vanished. Thel rnations are with us in our sorrow, and they join in ,our hymns and lamentations. We are not alone.

"To the feast of the good will go the good o their own accord." It is so with the funerals of ithe noble. The nations group around. They fee that the death of QuEzrr VICTORIA has been a ibereayement to them all.

1 Tsa Duita of NOR- The Temporal Power. FOLK has very cleverly

explained away his allu- sion to the Temporal Power. We have almost lanticipated the line he would take. He thinks that some arrangement should be come to between the POPE and the Italian Government, by which the former should have restored to him something that was taken away when Italian unity was achieved. What that arrangement should 130 he does not define, and no British Catholics ever have described,it. Probably they mean one thing, and the POPE'S entourage mean something else entirely different, and much more ambitious. That has ,always been the difficulty in dealing with the !Roman question. For instance, there may be all the difference between a restoration and an agree- ment. A restoration means the surrender of Rome to the Porn and the States of the Church which were grouped about Rome, by which the authority of the Italian Government would cease in these territories. It means the sub- stitution of an ecclesiastical for a civil government, and it was against the former that the Italians themselves took up arms, and might do again. It Would mean either that the soldiers of the Italian Government,:or the specially recruited soldiers of the POPE, or the soldiers of !France, or soma:other external Power, would have to protect the POPE against his own subjects and against the revolutionary influences certain to be developed outside the States of the Church by the presence in Italy of a kingdom in which civil government and civil guarantees would have ceased. Agreement may be intended to mean that the Italians should give up Rome to the POPE allow him to be styled King of Rome as well as ?OPE, and find some other capital. The King of Italy would not be King of Rome, and the King of Rome would not be King of Italy. The difficulties and dangers of such a state of things can be readily imagined. Unless the POPE were effieiently guarded, there would soon be a revolution in Rome, and probably another triumvirate. or republic there. There is another matter to be mentioned 4 The more ardent advisers of the Porn would like 'to see him take rank as King in Italy, and exercise over Catholic countries the kind of dictatorship, which existed in past ' times, when, as Vice-regent of God, the monarchs of other countries were ordered about by him as vassals and paid him tribute. It is advisable to be • plain on this subject. Protestants do not want to see

this revival, and they will do all they can to avert and resist it. It would be a melancholy experience for the world. But the Roman Church has its unquenched ambition in this direction, and hence we distrust all these playings with vague phrases like "the restoration of the Temporal Power," and the Vice-regency of the Pope. At 'bottom they mean undoing the work of the Refor- 'tion. They are designed to seduce the minds Protestants, and so disturS the whole of the (arrangements of the present civilised world. The. of NORFOLK may or may not clearly appre . tend what is behind, but intelligent publicists - mow very well what the Roman • Church is after. It seeks to recover its old sway over the monarchs,. the countries; and ths peoples of the world. tt aims at restoring the POpedom to what it was in its most powerful days. Hence the sturdy lay- man will not yield an inch of the ground which has been gained for thought and liberty in the struggle -tf the centuries.

lA. conclusive proof,if one were needed,of the ex. I,tent to which loyalty to the Queen had permeated the nation is to be found in the many references to sr Majesty in the highest poetry of the era. From ier accession to the very day of her death the grestest poets had offered her in the exercise of their art no lip service, but a devotion obviously deep and sincere. He predecessors had been the subject of many flattering addresses. A sucession of Poets Laureate—not now considered very illus- trious, yet doubtless highly esteemed in their day ./ad been accustomed to present .birthday odes co the throne ;. but of these what single line has stood the test of Time? But one of ,these poems may be said now to live, land that, a specimen of the handiwork of Pye,,survives merely as .an example of turgid diction and insincere feeling. With good sense, the annual custom of the birthday ode had been abandoned before the late Sovereign came to the throne ;. but, none the less, her name has been en- shrined in poetry. some of which, it is safe to pro.. diet,, will last as long as any of the finest verse which has been written in her reign. Acollection of the best poems in praise of Queen Victoria would undoubtedly make an interesting and prob- ably a popular volume. And its contributors would be found to include not only distinguished writers who have enjoyed in their career what may be called Court favour, but the young bards—the passionately democratic singers, the almost revol- utionary poets, of the past half-century.

IThe arrangements which are being made at ;Glasgow for the International Exhibition to be 'held there this spring, are now fairly complete. At all events, some idea may be gathered of the extent and importance of the show which will be provided in the Scottish city for all who will come to see; and it is possible, in no mere sense of corn- pliment, to congratulate the Corporation on its enterprise, and on the degree of success which seams to be assured. To have obtained the active support of fourteen or fifteen Governments more than justifies the designation of International and there are features which at the present moment make this outside co-operation particularly inter- esting. The United States, for instance, is sending to Glasgow—so important as an engineering centre—a great exhibition of machinery which is sure to attract widespread attention. Further, it is gratifying to note that from Russia has come the most effective foreign support, the Czar's Government having practically bought up the whole floor space which could be assigned to them and that France comes next, something like four 1 hundred private exhibits being expected horn that country. For the accommodation of these ex- hibits handsome buildings have been erected, iln.deed, that no detail should be left out in the scheme, the Glasgow Corpurltion, with national Icompleteness, has even decideu on waat object the profit to be derived from the show is to be spent. ift is to go towards the building of the great art !gallery commenced with the £54,000 representing re proceeds of the Exhibition of 1888, a work which, it is hoped, may soon be completed, at a ,cost of nearly a quarter of a million.

The Clerk of the Warrington Board has' an easy task. The circular received very brief consideration at Warrington, for if we remember rightly, it was dismissed with the suggestive re-: mark that the recommendations had been already, carried out by the Warrington Board. For manyi years prior to the receipt of the circular the maini suggestions have been in active operation in the -Warrington Union and the Local Government Board is therefore just a trifle late in the day. The Clerk has now drawn up a reply incorporating these facts. One would have thought that the central authority would have been cognisant of them, seeing that all the schemes adopted have received their approval.

One of the best ways of stopping the war would, Undoubtedly, be to stop the "Stop the War Com- mittee," which, individually and collectively ap- pears to have lost its mental balance. What ad vantage the committee can see in painting the British soldiers as black as possible is a mystery. We suppose it is public support the Committes de- sires above all else and yet they go to the furthest extreme in methods calculated to alienate that support. There was a" Stop the War" meeting on Thursday evening, and the principal argument was an accusation against Lord Roberts and Lord Kitchener of harbouring a policy having as its aim the extermination of a "heroic nationality" by starving its women and children and by the deliberate massacre of un- armed prisoners. A subsequent resolution re- ferred to "calculated atrocity" anti Great Britain "the enemy of the human race." Could arything be more childish or peur- ile, than these hysterical and venomousl attacks on officers and men whose conduct and; careers will bear the closest investigation F The! "Stop the War" agitators may perhaps considerl themselves exponents of a higher form of patriot- ism, but the man in the street has an estimate of them at his tongue-tip which be, like one of the, characters in Barry Pain's "Eliza," could expressil in two incisive, unanswerable words.

MATTERS OF MOMENT. A Provincial Letter. There has been but one topic of interest, or of Public concern, during the present week; the one Shbject upper-most in everybody's mind being the; fatal illness of England's Queen. Not only in Lon-I don, but in every town and village of the provinces' the name of our late ,evered Sovereign has been on everybody's lips, both young and old. And not only in this country, bur, in all parts of the world, the thoughts of people have instinctively turned to the sick bed at Osborne. The news, first of the grave condition of her Majesty and soon afterwards of her death, cast a feeling of general gloom over the people, and everywhere public engagements haNe;been cancelled and social functions abandoned The Victorian era, which has now terminated, has been the most marvellous in the life of this country It has been mark-d by transformations more strik- ing and glorious than those that might be brought about by the Oriental magician's wand; and in al- most every sphere of life have these wonderful changes been affected. Sixty-four years since, the lives of provincial people were for the most par. dismal and dreary. Toe conditions under which the majority laboured were not favourable to com- fort. Educati ,n was the luxury of the well-to-do. . Books were scarce; intellectual agencies woefully Circumscribed. The postal system was in if s in- ', fancy, and a heavy embargo lay on the newspaper Press. Railways were of a very elementary char- acter. There were no telegraphs, no telephones, no Tiontgen rays, and electric motors were not even dreamt of. What was, perhaps, worse for country People generally, was the fact That food and cloth- ing alike were dear. Houses were built with no .T,lgard to sanatation ; indeed sanatation, or sanita- il9u,was an unknown science. But within the leterian era these matters have changed amaz- ' ugly. The liberation of the newspaper press, the ! Zfoduction of cheap literature in book and maga- , chile form, the repeal of the Corn Laws, and the "Pening of food; the perfection of the penny Rost, the creation of a network of railways all over Kingdom, the extension of political rights by Reform Bill, the creation of a State and rate- aided education, the emancipation of workpeople ,bY Factory Acts, and of the mill hand from an un- healthy environment; the almost miraculous advances in medicine, surgery, and sanitation, and the startling discoveries made in various scientific fields—have all helped to render the people p throughout the country happier, healthier, and more self-reliant. During Queen Victoria's bene-I Ecent reign the wooden walls of old England have been superseded by those mighty fightinz engines lof steel which some day may be required to main- p. tain an uninterrupted supply of cheap food. In , short the Victorian era bristles with points of it thrilling interest.

--- An admirer of the" good old days," which hel seems to remember, when he says that beer was recognised as the product of barley-malt, and hops, and Water. desires that the public should be re- lainded of another matter, other than beer, to which "e thinks mlre attention should be given.
