DEATH OF AN OLD RUNCORNIAN. We regretfully record the death of MrJohn John- son, who up to rec-ntly acted as Assistant Magis- trates' Clerk at Burton-on-Trent, which took place bout 5 o'clock on Wednesday morning at his resi- ience at Burton. Mr Johnson was the son of ths Late Mr Stanley Johnson, and brother of Mr Frank Johnson. Clerk to the Runcorn Bench of Magis- trate. For many years he was Assistant Clerk to the Runcorn Magistrates, in the time of Mr John H Chorlton, and when he removed to Burton his brether succeeded him. The deceased gentleman was 54 years of age, and leaves a wife and two chil- dren to mourn his loss.—The funeral will take place at Burton-on-Trent next Saturday. ST PAUL'S .MOTHERS' MEETING, RUNCORN. In connection with the St. Paul's Mothers' Meet- ing, a very interesting afternoon was spent in the minister's vestry on Monday under the presidency of Mrs Robinson. There was a moderate attend- ance, and the president pro tem., after apologizing for the absence of Mrs Wells, the superintendent minister's wife, which it was explained was due to a somewhat severe illness, but from which,happily, ebe was rapidly recovering, delivered a most in- structive address, based on the words " Certainly I will be with thee." At the close of the meeting the members expressed the pleasure at seeing Mrs Robinson, the late president, with them once more, and hoped that she would often, when her health permitted, pay them a visit. DEATH

OF THE

QUEEN.

It is with profound regret that we record the death of her Most Gracious Mojesty, the Queen, which occurred at Osborne on Tuesday evening. The first intimation of the melancholy intellig- ence came to the Lord Mayor of London from the Prince of Wales by a telegram despatched from Osborne at a quarter to seven, as follows : My beloved Mother, the Queen, has passed away, surrounded by her children and grandchildren. ALBERT EDWARD, Osborne 6,45. _

I It was past seven o'clock when the official bulle-1 tin announcing the sad news was posted at ,Osborne House. It read as follows :

January 22nd, 1901, 6.45 p.m,—Her Majesty the Queen breathed her last at 6.30 p.m.,surrounded by her children and grandchifdren. (Signed) JAMES RRID, M.D. R. Dolmas Powslat M.D. THOMAS BARLOW, M.D. The Lard Mayor sent the following reply to the Prince—a reply which will be echoed throughout ithe land:—

Your Royal Highness's telegram announcing the nation's great loss I have received with pro- found distress and grief. I have communicated this most sad intimation to my fellow-citizens. Her Majesty's name and memory will live for ever in the hearts of her people. May I respect. fully convey to your Royal Highness, and all the members of the Royal Family, the earnest sym pathy and condolence of the City of London in your great sorrow.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LAST MOMENTS.

Though the Queen's illness was only made known, on Saturday its gravity quickly impressed itself on the public mind. Every succeeding hour increasedl the general anxiety, especially as the frequent bulletins gave ground for only the slightest hope„ Her Majesty rapidly worsened throughout Sunday, ,and Monday, and Tuesday, but retained conscious- ness to the last. After waking from the slumber into which she had fallen about noon on Tuesday she had so sharp an attack about half-past three that all her family had to be summoned from their various rooms, for none of them had left the pre- cincts of the Castle as they had done on Monday, knowing that the end could not be far off. Her wonderful vitality, however, again asserted itself and the sharp crisis was again surmounted, though, the august sufferer now began to sink slowly, andi her release was seen to be only a question of an hour or 80. Again the sorrowing circle of watchersi retired for a little, but presently they were called! back to the chamber of death, and in the midst ofi her anguished children and grandchildren, Victoria,l the great and good, serenely passed away to the presence of the Bing of Kings, leaving behind her a tharacter and a name of which the memory will sever die. The Duchess of York and her children, reached the Castle just in time for the end, but' Prince Christian was too late. Prince and Princess' Louis of Battenberg were among the bedside mourners, who included all the members of the Royal Family now in the country. The Prince of Wales was very much affected whet the doctors in waiting ac last informed him that his royal mother had breathed her last, and the scene was a most affec6ing one, almost beyond the power of words. Deeply affected himself, the Ger- man Emperor did his best to minister comfort to his sorrow-stricken uacle, whose new dignity he was one of the first, if not the very first, to acknowledge. During the last moments of Her Majesty the Bishop of Winchester and the Rev. Clement Smith read special prayers in the Queen's room, and later in the evening the Bishop conducted a short ser- vice in the Queen's death-chamber, at which all 1 the members ot the Royal Family at Osborne were present.

In the Death Chamber.

On Wednesday, the servants and retain ors of the late Queen at Osborne were permitted to see the remains of ltbeir beloved mistress. The scene was one which will long remain as a precious recollection in the memory of those who had the privilege of witnessing it. These A were , besides the servants the tenants on the Osborne Neste

The Queen.—A Memory.

{BY A LADY CONTRIBITTOIII

lOn Tuesday evening news came to us of the, IQueen's death. Since Saturday, when the first official bulletin was issued, we have all feared the ' worst, but hoped for the best. And now the worst .has COMO and our fears are realized, and the first, snsation is one of shock. Then .a sadness comes' over 1.18, and we feel as though we had lost a friend. For, above all, else the Queen's thoughtful kindli- ness, and ready sympathy have endeared her to her subjects all the world over, and the world will not be the same without her. We shall read our nswepapers, and miss the ever-recur- ring paragraphs relating to her generosity, and the interest and affection that prompted her to wire her sympathy in such touching terms to those who were in trouble. She was a woman first, and a Queen afterwards. All her life we have loved her, but never so much as during the last twelve months, and who shall say to what extent those twelve months are responsible for her death? We have sung " God save the Queen" with tears in, )ur eves and Jumps in our throats ; and now there no Queen and we find it very difficult to put the .ing in her place. It was the writer's privilege, 4wenty-iix years ago, when living not far from Buckingham Palace, to see the Queen on several foccasions, one of which has never been forgotten. While walking along Buckingham Palac- road, the Queen's carriage, a victoria, was seen coming in the opposite direction, with its grey horses, and containing the Queen and* others. All the pedestrians stood still while it passed, the gentlemen raised their hats, and the ladies bowed. Two ladies,evidently strangers, asked Whose carriage is that ?" " The Queen's," I said; I "Is she in it? " was the next question. " Yes,"l . answered, " the little lady in black is the Queen."l " What an ordinary-looking person she is !'' the)/ exclaimed. Yes,she was ordinary-looking and that is. why we loved her. We felt that she wasn't tar an,il away above us,but one of us and extraordinary onlyl in those qualities which made her"the gentlest lady in the land,"and endeared her to us all for ever.

ILECTURE ON VEGETABLE FOODS AT WARRING roN.

Professor William Stirling, NI.D., c ntinued hie course, of lectures on "Food and Nutrition "in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo-street, Warriagton, on Tu .alay evening. As !the audience assembled, the sad news of the death of the Queeu was circulated and became the topic of conversa- tion. Promptly at the advertised time, the Lecturer mounted the platform and stated that what with the dismal weather outside an] the des :Anti m of soul which pervaded every member of the English community at the present time, it was a somewhat difficult task to interest them on al question of their daily bread. He wished them to consider some of the chief constituents lof vegetables. There was one thing which distinguished vegetable foods from animal foods and that was that they contained a very large amount of starchy sub- stances, known as carbohydrates. These vegetable foods might also, under certain circumstances, contain fatty matter in the form of oil, as in the case of nuts or in oily seeds. The lecturer alluded to some of the industrial uses of starch and facetiously referred to the starch powder made from rice which was used by many ladies for itnproving the com- plexion. Starch was derived from an extraoadlnary number of different plants. They might obtain it from the tubers of potatoes, from the pith of palms or from underground stems of other plants. It was a reserve material in plants against the day of hunger, and was stored stored up so that it might be made eof when the period of flowering commenced. It was not a substance on which plants lived day after day. Starches had also different characteristics. The starch grains from rice' were exceedingly small whilst those from potatoes, were large. In speaking of the composition of sterol-LI the lecturer demonstrated by a simple experiment with iodine, the presence of carbon in large quantities, and proceeded to speak of the value of starch as a food sub- stance. Potatoes, he pointed out, were valuable ad- juocts to other foods and were readily digestible when properly cooked. The next subject treated by the lecturer was that of sugars. He classified them into the following : cane sugar, grape sugar, glucose or Idextrose ; and milk sugar or lactose, Sugars, be said, contained an enormous quantity of carbon They were burned up in the body for the production of heat and mechanical work,and when taken in immoder- ate quantity, favour the formation and deposit of fat. Reference :was made to the two principal iugars. beet sugar and cane sugar. The manufacture of such a large quantity of sugar from beetroot, and, the rich subsidies paid by foreign governments to beet producers, had caused beet sugar to seri- c.usly interfere with our own cane sugar industry in the Wrst bales. The lecturer then pro- ceeded to refer to cereals or cultivated grains dealing especially with wheat, wheaten flour and bread. Although rich in solids, bread was poor in fat and salts. The difference between leavened and unleavened bread was explained, as well as the aeration of bread by the .action of carbonic acid In referring to oatmeal, mon- ition was made of its importance as a food. It contained a large amount of niitrielon but the proteids 1 present had no adhesive properties like the gluten of wheat, hence bread could tint be made from it, iThe percentage of fat was larger than in wheaten flour. it was easily creaked, and could be eaten with relish for long periods. Reference was also made to macaroni, vermicelli and other foods, the lecturer concluding an instructive lecture by a description of compressed vegets ables, which had beet) proved of great value in military! ,xpeditions and in tim s of emergeney.

THE KING.

PROCLAMATION

EDWARD VII.

The following appears in the" Court Circular": - ST, JAMES'S PALACR, JANUARY 23. The King held his first Council to-day at S 'James's Palace). Previously thereto the Lords of tt 'Council, of whom more than one hundred wei present, the Lord Mayor, aldermen, and oth. 'officials of the City of London, who at in their robes of office, and other noblemen .gentlemen who were alio present, approved a pi clamation proclaiming Ella Majesty as King Edwai VII. The Proclamation was signed by the members the Royal family present, the Archbishops Canterbury and York, a large number of other Priv Councillors, and by the Lord Mayor and othi representatives of the City. At the Council ,Majesty made a declaration, which was ordered be published, and His Majesty subsequently and subscribed the oath for the security of Church of Scotland. The Privy Council were then re-sworn of His Majesty's Most able Privy Council, and the Lord Chancellor, Lord President, and the Lord Privy Seal took oath of their respective offices." The King had scarcely gone away from 'alace before it became kGown how much 'rivy Councillors had been impressed with iimple declarations he had made. Ho had moved with emotion, and so had they who list The important thing to know was that he chosen to be styled Edward VII., and not Al I. He had produced no surprise, as the Qt did when she refused the first name of Alexanch The title he has chosen has the authority of tonic continuity, and with that advantage it volves the negation of the highest ambitions ,King might have to create rather than to preserve. But the reason the King gave for his -eboice was, one that will bring him an instant gift of his' people's gratitude. He was guided, he said, only by the Queen's wish. Albert—Albert the Good— was to be a name connected only with the dear memory of the Queen. Great crowds assembled in London to see the ,ling as he passed on his way to and from St. !James' Palace. The King was on all hands warmly Ibut quietly greeted. A memorial service was held lat St Paul's Cathedral.

THE KING'S SPEECH. The text of his Majesty's address was follows :

Your Royal Highnesses, My Lords and Gentlemen. This is the most painful occasion on which I shall 'ever be called upon to address you. My first and melancholy duty is to announce to you the death of my beloved mother the Queen,and I know hew deeply you, the whole nation, and I think I may say the whole world, sympathise with me in the irreparable loss we have all sustained. I need hardly say that my constant endeavour will be always to walk in her footsteps. In under- taking the heavy load which now devolves upon me, 1 am fully determined to be a constitutional -overeign in the strictest sense of the word, and as long as there is breath in my body to work for the Rood and amelioration of my people. I have resolved to be known by the name of Edward, which has been born by six of my ancestors. In doing so 1 do not under- value the name of Albert, which I inherit. from my ever-to-be-lamented, great, and wisel father, who by universal consent is, I think, de- servedly known by the name of Albert the Good, and I desire that his name should stand alone. In conclusion I trust to Parliament and the nation to support me in the arduous duties which, now devolve upon me by inheritance, and to which, [am determined to devote my whole strength dur- ing the remainder of my life.

In accordance with the constitutional require- coent that Parliament should meet immediately on,l the accebsion of a new Sovereign, both Housesl lassembled on Wednesday. In the Commons thel Speaker explained to the members that, by reasoni of the deeply lamented death of the Queqn, it had' ibecome their duty to take the oath of allegiance to her successor. Mr. Gully then took the oath "to be faithful and bear trueallegianca to His Majesty Sing Edward, his heirs and successors,according to law." The remainder of the sitting was taken up by the administration of the oath to other mem- bers, numbering 300, who were present. In the House of Lords the proceedings were of a similar 'character. The Lord Chance,lor first took the 'oath and subscribed the roll, and a number of !other peers followed him. Among them were the 'Duke of Connaught and the Duke of York. The 'House then adjourned until Thursday morning.

The Terms of the Proclamation. The following are the terms of the Royal Proclamation; read at St James's Palace and in the city :—" Whereas it has pleased Almighty God t call to His mercy our late Sovereign Lady, Queen Victoria, of blessed and glorious memory, by whose decease the Imperial Crown of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ir( land is solely and rightfully come to the high and might Prince, Albert Edward, we therefore, the Lords spiritual and temporal of this realm, being here assisted with those of Her late Majesty's Privy Council, with numbers of other principal gentlemen of quality, with ithe Lord Mayor, al leraisn, and citizens of ,L ;ndoc, do now, hereby, with one voice and consent or ltougue and heart, publish and proclaim that the high id mighty Priuce Albert Edward is now by the death of our late Sever, ign of happy memory become our only (lawful ant rightful liege, Lord Eda ard VII., by the grace of God King of the United Kingdom of Great luritain and Ireland. defender of the faith, Emperor of "India, to whom we do acknowledge all faith and con- istant obedience with all hearty and humble affection,' 'beseeching God, by whom Kings arid Queens do reign. ) bless the Royal Prince Edward VII. with long and Ippy years to reign over us. Given at the Court of St James this 23rd day of January in the year of our Lord, '1901."

RUNCORN NOTES. [By • CORIIESPONDRNT.]

The Free Churches of the district are putting for- ard their best efforts to meet with success in the Jming Simultaneous Mission, which is being held throughout the land. A special appeal has been is- sued bythe Rev A Wells(superintendent of the Wes- leyan Circuit),urging upon his flock the desirability )f them arising to their responsibilities. " Don't let ts forget," he says, " that we are the largest Non- nformist boiy, and have the greatest responsibil- ." The exhortation is one that the general body Wesleyans is not likely to forget, and with such .a energetic and enthusiastic pastor, as the Rev A Wells, at their head, they should have a marked influence for good throughout the district.

1111 ward

leoi

qcon)

'of 1

dan

A general visitation from house te house will be art of the programme, and will result, without Aoubt, in some spiritull good being accomplished. What would be of practical utility, also, I venture to suggest would be a general report compiled by the "district " or "house to house " visitors,whilst on their rounds, of the condition of the housing of Runcorn's poorer classes. It would be something concrete to work upon and might result in a special mission to house property owners of the district,or what would be better still peremptory notice from the District Council to the owners to put their houses in order. The poor w iuld then see that their spiritual advisers were to a great extent their temporal benefactors; the elevators of their minds and bodies. Thus even the most selfish would be nroachable, and a greater good might be delved.

The Balance Sheet of the Runcorn Liberal Club lon not present anything very rosy to the eyes of its members, but for all that it is highly satisfactory ,in many respects. On the whole, however, the kCltib has paid on the year's working and the adverse ,alance at the bank is, if not materially decreased, . slightly diminished amount. The Committee of iManagement may congratulate themselves upon the manner in which they have tried to meet the wants of all members, and for the splendid line they have ltaken in the literary and debating department. WARRINGTON HONORS ATTACK A CONSTABLE—SMART SENTENCE At the Warrington Police Court on Saturday, Peter Scully and John Whittaker, who were described as station loafers, were charged, the former with being drunk and disorderly in Horsemarket-street and assault- ing P.C., Clough the previous night, and the latter with obstructing the constable whilst acting in the execution of his duty—P C , Clough stated that he was on duty in Horsemarket-street when Scully came up to him and said he would do eighteen montba for him. He then struck witness in the face and kicked him on the leg, causing him to bleed profusely. He took the men into custody and Whittaker came upon the scene. Sculls' called to him for assistance,and in consequence of what Whittaker did Scully got sway. HARRIERS.

Warrington v. Crewe. Despite the unfavourable weather on Saturday, these teams met in their inter-club run at Warrington, and he locals had the satisfaction of confirming the result of he first meeting. The Warringtonians did not expect he visitors; and as a result several of the most prominent unners were absentees. Crewe, however, were very nthusiastic, and having made the journey they were de- er:mined that the return engagement should take place. Crewe mustered twelve runners, but Warrington's com- plement was only seven, so that it was decided to only count the first four in each team. Track was laid over heavy country through Arplev, and by way of Walton, and al difficult was the trail to fellow that a mile from home the leader, F Townley (Crewe) missed his way and lost what possible chance he had of gaining honour. The Warringtonians, however, allowed the visitor to take first position, but even this advantage did not assist his aide to avenge the first defeat. for Townley was followed in quick euccesaion by four Warringtonians, and the final result was victory for the home club by eight points. The Warringtonians finished as follows : —Macdonald 2, Parsonat7e 3, Price 4, and Mather 5. Sankey. The Sankey Harriers j )urneyed to Manchester on Saturday to fulfil their return engagement with the Man- chester Harriers. Trail was laid by way of Heaton Park ever a distance of about six miles. The trail-layers were frllowed after an interval of about twenty minutes, but after about half the distance had ben traversed, the trail was loot, the race therefore being declared off. The Sankey men were afterwards entertained to tea, and spent a very enjoyable evening.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Electric Light in Cairo-street C,Thapel Cairo street chapel is the first local place of worship to be lighted by electricity. The new illuminant was used for the first time on Sunday evening and the excellent effect obtained was favourably commented upon by the congvgation. The pulpit was occupied by ttv, R6v. A. Pearson of Oldham, who congratulated his bearers upon the introduction of the light. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.—Open Meeting.

Despite the steady downpour of rain on Sunday after- noon, there was a fairly large attendance at the open meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society, when members of both sexes were admitted. Mr. S B Stringer presided and in the course of his address he alluded to the ap- proaching simultaneous mission. It was a unique un- dertaking for there had been nothing tike it in the his- tory of Christendom. It would commence on the 16th ot February and continue until the 26th, and all the Free Churches throughout the country were looking forward to grand results. It would quicken the Church life of the country and it was hoped to bring many outside the chnrches into them.— The address was delivered by Mr. J. E. Balmer, of Eccles, who gave some homely hints on how to obtaia happiness. The Orpheus IQuartet party, consisting of Messrs. ecattergood, Daven- portt Whitehouse, and Sherlock contributed two pieces in their usually efficient manner, entitled, "Pray for the peace at Jerusalem" and "The Long Day Closes." A collection was taken on behalf of the society's funds. The service was also taken part in by Mr. C. Eccles and Mr. Farrar.

Bishop Chavasse at Warrington. On Sunday morning, the Lord Bishop of Liverpool (Dr Chavassel preached at the Warrington Parish Church. There was s large congregatfon, the Bishop basing his discourse upon the words ;—" And when they wanted wine the mother of Jesus Bahl] unto Him, they have no wine. Jesus Bath unto her. Woman, what have Ito do with thee ? Mine hour is not yet come," His Lord- Ship gave an address in the afternoon in the church to all the Sunday school children of the parish. There was a large congregation. In the evening the Bishop preached to another crowded congregation in Holy Trinity Church from the words, " Let a man so account of us as of the ministers of Christ,and stewards of the mysteries ot God. Moreover, it is required in stewards that a man be fohnd faithful." His Lordship said he had been asked to speak to them on behalf of the Curates' Fund, and he would therefore remind the congregation as briefly and as clearly as he could of the nature of the Christian ministry, and of the duty of the laity towards them. A minister must not only be sacred and sympathetic but self-sacrificing, and there never was a time when the Church orEngland needed her clergy to be more self-sacrificing than at the present. They knew it. They did not shrink from it ; they were willing to take up the cross and deny themselves for the Lord's sake. Men of education, most of them, who had had hundreds of pounds spent upon them, were willing to accept a salary which many a clerk in Warrington would be unwilling to take for the sake of Elia] wet) died for them and for the sake of souls. In conclusion, what must be the attitude of the laity t.- wards the clergy ? They must pray for their minister, they must work for him. The time was long past when the congregation were satisfied for the vicar or curate to do all the work The secular and financial parts of the work of the parish ought to be done by the laity. They must not expect clergymen to do the work of the parish and preach good sermons. Sermons t iok hours of read- ing and study, and unless the laity gave the clergyman time for study they would never get the sermons they expected. Never until each c immunicant took an in- terest in the work of the church would the lapied masses be won for Christ and the Church of England become the Church cf the world. The rev, gentleman then referred in sympathetic terms to the Bishop of London, aad con- cluded by asking for a liberal donation to the fund. The New Vicar of Appleton—lnstitu- tion and Induction Ceremonies. There was a 'arge congregation at St Cross Church, Appleton Thorn on Tuesday evening, when the impor- tant ceremonies of instituting and inducting the new vicar (the Rev. F. E. Evans M. A.,) to the living, took place. The new incumbent was inetituted by the Lord Bishop of Chester (the Right Rev Dr Jayne) and the induction service was conducted by Archdeacon Barber. After a portion of the usual office of evening prayer had been gone thr nigh the Bishop preached an anpropriate sermon from the words "Make full proof of Thy Minis- try." He said that in God's earth and in God's church there were many mieistrieh some visible some invisible, coma high and wetanty, others, eimp a and humble, and they were joined together, ordained and con- stituted under one order and all of them were necess- ary for the carrying on of God's great purpos s After referring to different forms of ministry his Lordship SaLl they were at that time thinking with deepest sym- parlay and with, he VITAS sure, true ant earnest prayer about one great minister to whom, under God's order, was appointed the ruling of nationieTney were thinking of their Empress Q iren.Triey woeld remember the teach- ingot Holy Scripture upon that point. "The powers that be are ordained of God ", they were God's ministers, so that in one Collect they prayed for the Qaeres " that she knowing whose minister she is may above all things seek 'Fos Honour and Glory" They knew how the Qieen had beer' enabled through these long years most faithfully to fulfil her great ministry and they prayed God that even yet, by His Holy will, He might spare her a little longer to her Empire and in a very real sense to all the nations -of the world. Cominuin his Lordship dwelt upon the importance of the work carried on in country parishes, comparing it to a miniature picture ira which every detail had to be carefully rate's Seri ; to. He asked the congregation to pray that the new Vicar would mike full proof of his ministl amongst them.— The new incumtent then took th • WWI Oath of Allegi- ance to the Queen, and the Oath of Can° 'deal Obedience to the Bishop of the Diocese ; and m ado the Declaration of Ascent to the Thirty-nine Articles of Religion and the Book of Common Prayer ; and also the declaration. *varlet simony ; as -eqoired by law.—After several special prayers had been offered, the Bishop read the letters of institution and pronounced a Benediction.— The Archclescon receivieg at the hands of the Baahop the {dendrite of Induction, then proceeded with the church- wardens, and the newly-instityted priest to the church door, and, laying the hand of the priest; upon the key of the door, inducted Wm into "the real, actual,. end corporal posae,sion of the church, with all Or /Islets, profits, and appurtenences thereto belongin. g. —ln accordance with the usual custom the newl; inducted incumbent tolled the church bell t•, signify his taking possesaim of the church. "The Broken Melody" at Parr Hall. The first visit to Warrington of. M. Ao4ur:e Van Biene, the celebrated actor-musician, has been , attended with melancholy circumstances. On Mon- day, when the initial performance was given, the serious illness of the Q ieen was uppermost in the minds of everybxly and the attendance suffered in consequence. On Tuesday, the fateful news of the ' Queen's death arrived as the audience was assemb- ling, and it was decided that no performance Siould be given. Since then, the public grief at the loss of the Qleen has interfered with all kinds of amuse- ments. There was a striking and tnernorable scene at the performance on Monday evennag. During an interval, M. Biene acknowledged the flittering reception he had received. He proceeded to state that they were all pained to hear of the serious ill- ness of the Qieen, but he did not like to interfere with the entertainment and to play, as a gentleman in the gallery had praviou ;Iv suggested he should play, "God save the Q letift." No one regretted her Majesty's illness more than he did, and they all deeply sympathized with the Royal Family in the irightful news they had heard that morning. He hoped it would please Almighty God to prolong her life. He asked the audience to join with him in singing" God save the gotien," and, said he "Let us hope that, if she must be taken from us, at all events her last momenta may be peaceful." The audience then rose and sang with much feeling the first verso, the scene being a 4ery impressive one. It should be stated that X. Biene's visit to Warring- ton is due to the enterprise of Messrs Kilduff and Hardiman, and it is to be regretted that circum- stances have conspired to interfere with the success of the venture. The performance of M. Biene on Monday evening was admirable. Both as a musi- cian and actor he displayed arti4ic talents of a high order and woo the plaudits of an appreciative audience. His performance on the 'cello was marked with musicianly taste an I skill. M. Biene: is assisted by a capable company, Miss Lena Burnleigh very feeling sustained the role of Mabel Borenski's wife, end Miss Ellen Snow was equally auvessful in the unpopular part of the Duchess de Verviers. General -vanoff (agent of the Rulsian secret, Police) was represented by Mr Brie Mayne, who as a spy discharged his duties with marked ability. Mr C A Morgan and Mr John Nolan as Mr Dickson and Dudley Mortimer respectively were , deservedly favourites with the audience. Minor parts were taken in a prai,e worthy manner by the following: The Hon Richard ,Spinnaker, Mr H Bhck ; Doctor Nragrath, Mr Joseph E4perance; Bea- jour, Mr W C,ank ; Mrs Dickson, Miss Ida Reed ; Mrs Spinnaker, Miss C pipmae. MISSIONS.

The Call for Volunteers. Warrington's Response.

The meeting which was held in the Drill Hall 00 Friday evening was convincine of the fact that the Warrington Volunteers sII not be lacking in willingness to contribute their fair quota of men to serve their country in South Africa. Though the battalion has already furnished a company and a half for active service, there is no doubt that it will be able to provide the additional number of men which will be required from V.Varrington. An air of enthusiasm pervaded the proceedings, which were wit- nessed by a large number of the public. Colonel RIDGWAY briefly addressed the men. Ho said they would have seen from the orders that further com- panies were being formed to go out to South Africa and he had been asked by the War Office to form a com- pany. The first company which the battalion sent out had been at the front for about twelve months and it was thought it was time they should be relieved and another company should take their place. Ho had not yet received from the War Office, the conditions under which the men would go out, but so far as he knew it would be under similar conditions to the first company, that was that the men would not have to bear any expenses and the corn-e pan y weld be thoroughly equipped in every way. Hr had called them together that night to see what numbs of men were willing to go out. He could not say how many would be required, but he wanted as many as possible to volunteer to show what Warrington and the battalion could do. When they would go out he could net say as it would take some little time to get the necessary uniforms. As regarded age he did not know what the conditions would be bu'i he thought they would be as before. In conclusion he asked all who were willing to volunteer to step to the front. There was a splendid response to the appeal, no less ' than 104 mon stepping forward amid loud applause• Having inspected the ranks, the Colonel expressed his pteasure at seeing such a hearty response. He be- lieved other men, who were unable to attend the meet- ing had also given in their names. I The proceedings Concluded with cheers. ~

WARRINGTON PUPIL TEACHERS' CENTRAL DAY CLASSES.

A meeting in connection with these classes was held in] the Town Hall, on Friday evening. The Rev Morley Stevenson was voted to the chair. There was a large attendance.

REPORT AND BALANCE SHEET.

The CHAIRMAN expressed his regret at the absence of the Rev Canon Willis,who was the chairman of the com- mittee. He must off.-r an apology to the meeting for the fact that they had not had an annual meeting for two years. He could assure there that all he members of the committee were truly peed tent—(hear, hear)—and they earnestly trusted that -fleeting would give them absolution. (Hear, hear and laughter.) In giving a brief history of she classes, the Chairman referred to the supplementary classes which were previously held at the Training Coll( ge for the benefit of pupil teachers. The numb ir, however, out- grew the accommodation and the resources of the teach- ing staff, and it was decided to establish a centre for pupil teachers. Two years ago, Mr Dennison was op. Pointed head teacher and Miss Warburton,assist. teacher He'was sure he was only voicing the opinion ef the Committee when he said they were extremely indebted to Mr Dennison and feti39 Warburton for the earnestness, zsal, diligence and success with which they had carried on the classes through many disadvantages and seine discouragement up to the present time. (Applause.) In January, 1899, there were 70 pupils, in January, 1890, 88 pupils and in January, 1891, 102 pupils. (Applause.) So much had the centre increased that the Committee had bun it necessary to appoint another assistant teleher. (Hear, hear.) The Government Inspectors report for the year said " The premises are not first-rate, and will, I hope, at no very distant date, be superseded by some- thing better. The teachers work with much zeal, and spare no pains in their efforts to make the instruction successful. He hoped that as the centre increased ths committee would be able to provide mare suitable pre- mises, but they were bound by an agreement to stay at the present premises another year. With regard to tbe results obtained at the October examination, 23 were in the first class, five second class, 10 third class, and one failures. At the previous examinationthe results wer:3 four first class, 23 second class, 11 third class, and three failures. Mr DEWIILTRST presented the balanc3 sheet in the ab- sence of the hoe treasurer (Mr F Monks). The balance in hand, Including a prospective grant of £6O for draw- ing. is £9B 8s 11d. The report and balance Sheet were adopted on the proposition of the Rev B. V. Pigot, seconded by Mr Craik. A vote of thanks was tendered to Mr Monks for his services.

The Appointment of Managers. Letter fr om the Teachers' _Association.

The CRAIRM&N reported that he had received a com— munication frcm Mr Upson, the secretary of the Warrington Teachers' Association stating that that he had unanimously passed the following resolution: " The time has now come when it is desirable that the association of Warrington Teachers should te recognised by the authorities of the town in all matten Concerning education and should be represented on those bodies having to do wi.h education. In aceordancs with this resolution the writer continued,it is considered epuitabel that the head teachers should elt.ct their own represen. I tatives on the Management Committee of the Pupil Teachers Centre. It is believed that such elected repre- sentation will more accurately reflect the views and opinions of the Warrington Teachers than teachers nominated in a semi public meeting. It is thought best that the head teachers should, bsfore attending a meet- ing of managers and teachers, choose their own repre- sentatives and he prepared at the general meeting to I nominate their own caadidates for the committee. After discussion it was decided on the proposition of ' Mr COREY that the following gentlemen should repre- sent the bead teachers on the Committee of Manage- ment Desch:yet. Mr Edward Wallington, Mr Upson and Mr Stanslield• Tne following gentlemen were also appointed man- agers, in addition to tho head teachers enumerated above :—Rey Canon Willis, Rev Morley Stevenson, Rev H V Pigot, Rev John Vonge, Alderman F W Monks, Major Buckton, Councillor A J Jolley,Rev A 9 Cameron and Mr F W Monks, J.P., (hon treasurer). The meeting concluded with an acknowledgment of the Chairman's services.

*. ACCIDENT AT A WIRE ROPERY.

A Warrington Man Claims Compensation. What Constitutes a Factory? An important claim unler the Workmen's Com- pensation Act of 1897, was brought before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands at the Warrington Comity Court on Thursday last week. A young man named Charles Thomas Rigby, who resides at 14, David-at., applied for compensation for injuries sustained by him in the course Of his employment at the Warrington Wire Rope Works, Limited, Bewsey-road.—Mr Browne appeared on behalf of the applicant, and the respondents ware represented by Mr Leslie Scott, barrister, of Liverpool. It appeared that the applicant, who was stated to be : an assistant overlooker, was injured on the 25th of June last year, whilst replacing a belt on a pulley in the fine , wire room at the respondents' works. His clothing was caught in the adjoining pulley and he was taken round the shafting. le consequence of the injuries he sustained his left leg had to be amputated below the knee. The knee and ankle of his right leg, the thumb of his right , hand, his right eltiow and ribs were also injured. He was thus imminently disabled. Before the accident, it was stated by the applicant, he could earn 353 per week , and since the arci4ent he had been unable to follow his ' employment. He claimed compensation at the rate of 17s 61 per week from the 9th July 1900, to January 17th ' 1901, (£lB 12s 6,1 in all), and 17a 6d for the remainder of his life. Mr Browne said there was no dispute that it was t,, appicant's duty to put on or take off the belt and understood that the defence was that toe accident not happen in the ordinary course of his employmen ' that be was guilty of wilful misconduct, and that h did not earn me wages which ha ;applicant) alleged' Mr Scott said the respondents would admit the liabil- ity, subject only to the question of the amount of com- pensation. The firm had from the outset offered the man ' half of what they:eaid were his average weekly earnings, and they were still prepared to give that amount. They admitted the liability and would accept the judgment of the court in respect to the amount. Tne reason for them taking this course was that the applicant refused to accept the offer and they regarded the accident as the result of what they thought was misconduct within the meaning of the Act. He (Mr Scott) bid inspected the ' place and considered the circumetances, and he was of Opinion that although it was gross negligence, applicant', , conduct was not wilful misconduct within the exact meaning of the Act and he was advised to admit the liability. His Honour : Do you dispute Van amount he earns ? Mr Scott : Yes, we say his average weekly earnings were 17s 2d. The applicant was an ordinary workman and not an assistant overlooker. We offered to take him back as soon as he was well, at his old wages. Mr Browne said the man had a standing wage of 21s per week but he had drawn as much as 30s above his ordinary wages in overtime. Mr Scott said the man's wages were 3s 81 per day. He had a statement of the wages earned by him during the 12 months pre- vious to the accident, which included ordinary time and overtime in the factory. The reaeein for the discrepancy between the amounts was that the applicant earned a certain amount of extra wages not in the factory at all but in another business also c trried on by the respon- dents, which business was not a bulimia within the Compensation Act. He submitted that in calculating his wages the applicant was not entitled to take into account, overtime which was made in a business to which the Act did not apply. His Honour : What is the other business ? Mr. Scott : It is a business in which netting is made by hand. NI machinery at all is employed. The busi- ness by itself is one which would not come within the Act, and if the man had been injured by working at that business he would net have been entitled to compensa- tion at all. Mr. Browne contended very strongly that the applicant was entitled to take the overtime into account. The whole works was a factory and they could not say one part was a factory and another was not. It was certi- fied a factory by the Inspector. The injury was sus Wind on premises and in course of work within the Act. Supposing the man had said he would not do the over time, he would have been dismissed from the works. Re had worked overtime continuously for three years. Mr Browne rnentioled tho fact that the respondents had been exceedingly courteous to him and the overtime was the only. question between them. The applicant then gave evidence. He said he was an assistant overlooker, being paid 3s a week more than ordinary workmen. Cie went to work in the hand net- ting department at the request of Mr Chadwick, the manager of the works. The band-netting shed was about eight yards away from the place in which he was working before. There was only one entrance to the works, and the shed was within the wall which sur- rounded the whole of the premises. He had earned as much as 304 per week by the overtime, which be worked ever" night and on Saturday afternoons. The Factory Act regulations were posted up a:, the entrance of the works. Mr. Scott said that the twee wages includine time and overtime averaged 15u 21 per week. Applicant was therefore entitled to a maximum of 7s 7d. At this point,his Honour in order to facilitate matters decided to visit the works. On his return. Evidence was given by William Pimblett,He said the, worked in the netting sheds during mealtimes and after Ithey bad finished work in the factory at 8.30. They also worked on Saturday afternoons. Mr Scott : It is worse than a barrister's life. /rank Wright, a bench hand in the netting shed, said

THE QUEEN'S REIGN.

The following is a brief summary of the fm. portant events during the reign of Queen Victoria:—

Her Majesty was born at Kensington Palace on 24th May, 1819, being the only child of the Duke of Kent, fourth son of George 111. Her mother, who was a widow when she married the Duke, was Victoria Mary Louisa, daughter of his Serene Highness the Duke of Saxe-Coburg Saalfeld, and sister of Prince Leopold, King of the Belgians. Her bringing up was characterised by great sim- plioity. Hex playmate was the Princess Feodore, her half-sister. Before she was a year old she lost her father, who died through receiving a severe chili; but the young Princess enjoyed the inestimable blessing of having a thoughtful, wise, and loving mother. The Rev. George Davys, afterwards Bishop of Peterborough, was chosen as her preceptor. The Duchess of Kent early familiarised her illus- trious daughter with the features of her own country, interesting her in it by personal visits to its chief cities and towns. The Princess attained her legal majority on the 24th May, 1837. Her last appearance at Court as Princess Victoria was at the drawing-room in honour of the King's birth- day, May 29,1837.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
1537.-THE QUEEN'S ACCESSION.

The death of William IV. on 20th June, 1837, raised Victoria to the throne of the British Empire at the early age of 18. The young Queen was fortunate in possessing Ministers whose sound ad- vice she wisely deferred to. The Duke of Sussex and Viscount Melbourne were the chief of these, Lord Melbourne, who was then Premier, represent- ing what is now known as the Liberal party. The Queen's first Privy Council was held on the morning of the 21st.

The Princess Victoria was formally proclaimed Queen of Great Britain and Ireland on the 21st of June, from St. James's Palace, amid scenes of great rejoicing; and on the 17th July she paid her first visit to the House of Lords for the purpose of pro- roguing Parliament. Her Majesty was attired in a splendid white satin robe, with the ribbon of the Garter crossing her shoulder, and a magnificent tiara of diamonds on her head. From the first the Queen entered fully into all business matters brought before her by the Prime Minister. She would know the why and the wherefore of everything. Indeed, one authority says that Melbourne was heard to declare that he would rather have 10 kings to manage than one Queen.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
1838.

Her Majesty's coronation took place on 28th June of this year. Preparations were made for it on a scale of unusual magnificence, although the actual ceremony was shorn of many ancient and expensive features. On the eventful morning the streets were crowded with people to view the imposing procession. The scene inside West- minster Abbey was a most brilliant one. Observed of all ebservers moved the girlish figure of the Queen, calm, self-possessed, and placid advancing with conscious dignity and a firm tread, a little pale, and very solemn. There, amidst the mightiest of the living, and surrounded by the grandest memorials of the mightiest dead, the fair girl, from whose brow the sunny gaietyof childhood had scarcely passed away, took upon herself the awful responsibilities of empire. To the corona- tion succeeded great festivities all the country over. 1839.

This was in many ways a famous year. In it was passed the Act for suppressing slave-trading, and slavery was abolished in the West Indies; in it the great Corn Law political agitation, which had commenced in the preceding year, attained more formidable proportions; in it the Govern- ment determined to adopt Rowland Hill's penny postage system. The railway system was by this time making enormous strides, and steam naviga- tion began, for the first time, to assume its real inteinational importance. Troubles were threaten- ing Canada, an Anglo-Indian army was fighting in Asia, and the Chartist disturbances were causing much uneasiness at home. Riots in connection with these occurred in Lancashire and elsewhere and had to be suppressed by the military. In 1839, too, a fearful hurricane raged along our coast, destroying enormously lives, ships and property. Then there was the important event of the Queen's betrothal to her cousin his Serene Efigbness Prince Albert of Saxe-Coburg Gotha. The Prince had acquired a reputation, not only as a scholar, but as a man of science, and it was a rod day for England when he came to guide with his sagacious counsel and his high moral character the lady who sat on the throne.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
1840.

This year was notable as that in which New Zea- land became a British colony, and on the 27th April the foundation-stone of the new Houses of Parliament was laid. It also saw the inauguration of the Penny Post. The Tithe battle was raging in Parliament at this time; vaccination was then first enforced, and the barbarous custom of chim- ney-sweeping by children under 16 years of age was done away with. The Royal marriage was solemnised at St. James's Palace on the 10th February. The wed- ding ceremony was one of unusual interest, for more than a century had elapsed since the nuptials of a reigning Queen of England had been cele- brated, besides which the youth and grace of Vic- toria had touched all loyal hearts. The Queen wore on her head a wreath of orange blossoms and a veil of Honiton lace, with a necklace and earrings of diamonds. Her dress was of white satin, with a very deep trimming of Honiton lace, in design similar to that of the veil. The body and sleeves were richly trimmed with the same material, to correspond. The train, which was of white satin, was trimmed with orange blossoms. The cost of the lace alone on the Queen's dress was £l,OOO. Both Houses of Parliament congratulated her Majesty on her choice of a Consort. Lord John Russell moved that an annual sum of £50,000 be granted as a provision to Prince Albert, but Mr. Joseph Huxue said that £21,000 per annum would be quite enough; and ultimately an amendment by Colonel Sibthorp that the grant should be for £30,000 was carried, being supported by Sir Robert Peel and most of the Conservative members. Neither her Majesty nor her husband expected to find life easy in their exalted station. The Court rejoicings had been denounced by the Chartists as heartless and wasteful in a year of distress, and influenced thereby, perhaps, a wretched, half-crazy barman, a mere lad, named Edward Oxford, who, while on Constitution Hill on 10th June, fired a pistol at her Majesty, as she was riding by with the Prince. Oxford fired a second shot, but it, like the first, passed over her Majesty's head. The man was seized by those who stood near. He was tried, pronounced mad, and sent to Bedlam.

The first child—the Princess Royal—was born to the Queen at Buckingham Palace on 21st November of the same year.

I'

1841. Perhaps the most remarkable event of this year was the change of Ministry, consequent on the de- feat of the Whig Government, Sir Robert Peel taking Lord Melbourne's place as Prime Minister. The Queen's parting with Lord Melbourne was a' very trying one on both aides, her Majesty regard- ing her Premier as "a faithful and attached friend as well as Minister."

On November 9, Lord Mayor's Day, the Prince of Wales saw the light, and on the birth of an heir to the throne there was great rejoicing. The christening of the Prince of Wales in St. George's Chapel at Windsor was a very imposing ceremony. Probably there never was a period in her Majesty's life when she was more )fonndly happy than at

pro' this time. 1842. This year was a troublous one. The blind paa- 'ions of political partisans found vent in riot and destruction. The distress amongst the labouring poor W2A worse than_ever..atrikea abounded in .the

manufacturing districts, there was war In China and rebellion in India, and the Corn Law agitation at home added to the political strife. Trade was at a standstill. It was not, therefore, without a shadow over her happiness that the Queen opened Parliament in person on the 3rd of February. Two attempts to assassinate the Queen were made this year, both while she was out driving. Her Majesty's first visit to Scotland— the land she learned to love so well—was made this year.

1843. By this time the Afghan War was nearing Its end. The war with China terminated in a com- mercial treaty between the two countries, the consequences of which have tended to mutual advantage and prosperity. Ireland was on the verge of rebellion, and the Repeal party under O'Connell were holding what were then first named Monster meetings. The Rebecca riots were commenced in Wales, directed against toll-bars, and carried on with singular success and secrecy, in defiance of both soldiers and police. In this year another daughter was born to the Queen— Princess Alice Maud Mary. 1844. _ _

One of the chief events of this year was the visit to the Queen of Louis Philippe, Bing of the French. Her Majesty's Imperial godfather, Nicholas, Czar of All the Russias, was also in London this year. On the 6th August the Queen gave birth to a son at Windsor Castle, who was christened Alfred Ernest Albert, being afterwards created Duke of Edinburgh.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
! 1, ' ',l _r --

1845.

An abundant harve;,-;astlimprovements in commerce, trade, and manufactures, railway enter- prise assuming gigantic proportions—these were the hopeful signs of the new year. The settlement of the great Corn Law question was seen to be imperative towards the close of 1845, and Sir Robert Peel resigned office in order that Lord John Russell and the Whigs might come in and grapple with this vexed question. Lord John was unable to form a Ministry, however, and on the sth of December Sir Robert Peel returned to power. He courageously resolved to abolish the Corn Laws, and although by doing so he incurred great odium with his party, the country generally acknowledged with gratitude his great and disin- terested services.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
1846.

The debate on the question which ushered in the new year lasted for a number of nights, and was of a deeply historic character. But, although Mr. Disraeli said bitter things of his changed opinions, Peel triumphed, the Bill passed the third reading by 327 votes against 229, and the Corn Laws were swept away. The state of Ireland was becoming a very alarming one. On the 25th of May baby fingers once more appeared in the Royal ,nursery, and Princess Helena Augusta Victoria took her place in the affections of the 'English people.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
1847.

The resignation of Sir Robert Peel took place. At Windsor corn was sold for £3l a load. This country was not the only sufferer; food riots and death from starvation were frightfully common on the Continent.

In this year the Royal family made a tour round the West Coast of Scotland on board the yacht Victoria and Albert. The Queen also spent some days at Hatfield House, _

1848. This was a stormy year, witnessing as it did an upheaval among the States of Europe. In our own country the General Election of 1847 had given a new Ministry and a Budget, which, showing a serious diminution of the public revenue, necessi- tated an addition to the income-tax. This raised a fierce storm of popular indignation, and it had to be withdrawn. Chartist disturbances again took place. Under one Feargus O'Connor a great gathering was held on Kennington Common, which threatened to be so serious that soldiers were called out and held in readiness, unseen, but under arms; 15,000 special constables were sworn in, shops were closed, artillery prepared, &c., &c. It ended in the dispersion of the Chartists without blows, and the reception in Parliament of a huge petition, which was found to have, not as had been so noisily asserted, five millions of signatures, but under two millions, and of these a large number were fictitious. Thus ended what was to have been the great English Revolution. Another child was born to the Queen amid those troublous time—her fourth daughter, Princess Louise. 1849.

The most important occurrence that this year witnessed was the Queen's long-expected visit to Ireland. She was given a very enthusiastic wel- come to Irish soil, and during their tour through the country the Royal family were received with acclamation and loyally appreciative speeches. And this although the land was still suffering from distress and political commotion. The cholera made awful ravages this.year. In September prayers for its removal were offered up in all the churches, and in some distriota days of humiliation were appointed. It was estimated that up to October 2 the deaths by cholera registered in London were 13,161, and. the /sth of the same month was ordered by Royalwo- clamation to be observed throughout the kingdom as a day of thanksgiving for the e-Agsation o the scourge. 1350.

In the merry month of May Prince Arthur, now Duke of Strathearn and Connaught, was bona. .

The middle year of the century was likewise marked by the deaths of two distinguished men— Sir Robert Peel, an old and tried friend of the Queen, and one of her chief advisers for many years; and his Royal Highness the Duke of Cam- bridge, the bluff old uncle of her Majesty. 1851.

This year will ever be memorable as that in which was opened by her Majesty the first of our great International Exhibitions, a lasting, solid, and substantial benefit to the nation, and for which a debt of affection and gratitude will ever be due to its Royal projector, Prince Albert. After all its cost was paid a surplus remained of £150,000, which was subsequently spent in the purchase of that land on which now stands South Kensington National Galleries and Museums. A striking feature of the year was the excitement created by the discovery of gold in Australia, and between the February of 1849 and the end of the year 1851 there was such a rush of emigrants from this country to the Australian gold-fields as had ever before been witnessed. Lord John Russell's Administration resigned, but in obedience to the Queen's wishes again took office.

1852.

The Reform Bill was introduced by Lord John Russell, who a few days later resigned, and the Derby Administration succeeded. After enjoying a pleasant trip to the Continent, the Court removed to Rzlmoral, where the Queen was deeply grieved to receive inteltigence of the death of the Duke of Wellington at the patriarchal age of 83. In December the Derby-Disraeli Government fell upon its Budget. Lord <-6erdeen became Prime Minister, Mr. Gladstone being Chancellor of the Exchequer for the first time. 1853.

The eighth child of her Majesty, and her fourth son—Leopold, afterwards Duke of Albany—was born in April. At the end of August the Queen and Prince Albert, with the Prince of Wales and Prince Alfred, crossed over to Dublin, to visit the Exhibition of Art and Industry which had been opened in the Irish capital. The Royal visitors remained in the Irish capital for a week, evincing great interest in the various native manufactures and industries.

The Crinlman War broke out, and all the country was plunged into feverish excitement as to the doings in the Crimea. Her Majesty felt very keenly the haedships endured by the troops and the prolonged siege of Sebastopol, and she was deeply grieved at the blundering and mismanagement that there went on. Bread riots occurred over the country in conse- quence of the price of the loaf being raised from 4d. to 9d. Lord John Russell again introduced the Reform Bill.

1855. Lord Palmerston was called to power on the re- signation of Lord John Russell. The Queen watched the events with the most eager interest, and she and all her anbjects rejoiced when Sebasto- pol fell ou 8 Lit oeptember. In April her Majesty received a visit from her Imperial ally, the Em- peror of the French, and in August the Queen VIM in Paris.

1856. On the last day of January her Majesty opened Parliament in person under auspicious circum- stances. Shortly afterwards the war was at an end. The Victoria Cross was founded, being east from metal of Russian guns.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
1857.

In July of this year the news of the Sepoy rising In India fell like a. thunderclap, with its scenes of violence an revengeful, barbarous cruelty, to be sternly repressed by the gallant soldiers under Sir Colin Campbell and Sir Henry Havelock. There were also hostilities with China. The Princess Beatrice was born on April 14th. 1858.

The event in the circle at Windsor this year was the marriage of the Princess Royal to Prince Frederick William of Prussia. The Queen this year visited Birmingham. The feverish interest with which the progress of the war against the Sepoy rebels was followed in England continued to prevail. News of the death of Havelock caused the deepest grief, but the close of the year saw the final suppression of the Mutiny. The war in China also c•me to a fortunate end.

There was again a change of Governmei,t, Lord Derby taking office. 1859.

This was an exciting year. France and Austria engaged in a bloody conflict, of which Garibaldi was the hero.

The Ministry were several times defeated on the Reform Bill, and at last gave way to Lord Palmer- ston, who again came into office, and remained Premier for several years. The Queen's first grandchild was born at Berlin in January of this year, herself being only 40 years of age. 1860.

The Prince of Wales visited North America, where he was enthusiastically welaomed by all except a few riotous Orangemen. In the United States his Royal Highness net with a reception hardly less satisfactory. 1861.

The Queen now lost her dear mother, the Duchess of Kent, an event which threw her into great grief. But a greater trial was in store for her Majesty, for before the year was out she was a widow. Everywhere there was long and genuine mourning for the "blameless Prince." Lord John Russell took his seat in the Upper House as Earl Russell. Disturbing influences arose abroad, the chief being the outbreak of the Civil War in America. Ireland was for a third time hotioured with a visit from her Majesty, the Prince of Wales being at this time serving in the Curragh Camp. 1862.

The American Civil War, bringing disaster to Lancashire this year in the shape of a Cotton Famine, let politicians see that it would not do to neglect our colonial trade in the interests of foreign trade. In February of this year the Prince of Wales went on a lengthened tour in the East.

1863. This year is memorable as that which saw the heir to England's throne united in wedlock to the "Sea-king's daughter from over the sea," the Princess Alexandra. The marriage took place on 10th March at Windsor, amid great rejoicings in every corner of the land. The July of this year saw the Princess Alice united in marriage to the Prince of Hesse.

In October the Queen went to Aberdeen to un- veil a statue of the Prince Consort. The tomb which the Queen erected for her beloved husband cost upwards of £200,000, which was entirely de- frayed from her Majesty's privy purse.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
1864.

A joyful but unexpected event occurred at Frog- more on the Bth January, when the Princess of Wales was prematurely confined of a son, Prince Albert Victor. . .

—.., ...,—.. A war which had been raging for some time be- ween Germany and Denmark was brought to an end. 1865.

It was in this year that the American Civil War came happily to a close and President Lincoln was shot through the held while sitting at his box in the theatre by an actor named Wilkes Booth. On October 18 died Lord Palmerston.

The year closed with a great personal loss to her Majesty in the death of her uncle, King Leopold. Richard Cobden also died.

1866. In February the Queen attended a state cere- mony for the first time since her husband's death, when she opened Parliament. Mr. Gladstone in- troduced a 'Reform Bill in March, but in Jane the Government had to resign. Then Lord Derby's third Administration commenced.

- G-r-e;tiiteft;m—cie—m—m;;t—rat—iols--;ere held through. out the country. 1867.

The Queen again came forth from her seclusion in February, 1867, when she once more opened Parliament in person. Fenian risings attracted considerable attention, resulting in the outrage at Clerkenwell Prison to rescue two members of the organisation, when great damage was done by the explosion. 1868. Lord Derby having resigned, Mr. Disraeli was appointed Premier. Later on Parliament was dis- solved, and as the elections resulted in a large Liberal majority Mr. Gladstone formed a Cabinet and took office. The Duke of Edinburgh visited Australia, where a Fenian attempt was made upon his life.

===== 0002601_19010125_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
1869.

This year saw the Irish Church disestablished by Parliament, and the formal opening of the Suez Canal. O'Donovan Roma was returned as member for Tipperary, but he was not able to take his seat. Among the deaths which brought sad- ness was that of the Earl of Derby. 1870 _ _

Opened with a frightful disaster at sea, caused by a collision between the Peninsular and Oriental Company's steamship Bombay and the United States corvette Oneida. Mr. Forster's Elementary Education Bill was introduced during this year's session.

Post-cards were issued for the first time; and another noteworthy incident was the conviction of Michael Davitt and his sentence to fifteen years' penal servitude. Charles Dickens, the great modern novelist, died this year. The year 1870 has its place in history strongly marked, for in it broke out the great war between France and Germany, which ended in the overthrow of the Napoleonic dynasty.

1871. The war rapidly came to an lend, and the King of Prussia assumed the title of Emperor of Ger- many. In this year was decided the famous Alabama case of arbitration between this country and America.

The Prince of Wines had a long and severe illness from typhoid fever at the close of the year, which at one time threatened to end fatally. 1872.

On 13th February a crazy Irish lad, maddened by the Fenian horror, threatened the Queen with an empty pistol to induce her to read a petition demanding the release of Fenian prisoners. In the same month a Thanksgiving Service took place for the recovery of the Prince of Wales, followed by a general illumination in the evening. A notable event of the year was the passing of the Ballot Act. In the beginning of the year Lord Mayo, Governor General of India, was assassinated, and was succeeded by Lord North- brook. 1873

Was a tranquil and prosperous year. A famine in India was amongst its sa=est events. In January Napoleon 111., who had taken up his residence amongst us, died, after a painful illness, at Chislehurst. The war in Ashantee was com- menced. 1874.

There was no striking event this year beyond a change in the Ministry. Parliament was unexpect- edly dissolved, and as the result of the elections placed Mr. Gladstone in an hopeless minority, he resigned, and Mr. Disraeli formed a Cabinet. At St. Petersburg in January was celebrated the marriage of the Duke of Edinburgh with the Grand Duchesse Marie Alexandrcvna, only daughter of the Emperor of Russia. In May the Czar, Alexander 11., visited England, and was everywhere greeted most cordially. 1875

Was a comparatively uneventful period. Mr. Gladstone retired from leadership of Liberal party, and Lord Hartington was selected in his place. The Prince of Wales left to make a tour through India; and in November Mr. Disraeli made his famous purchase of the Suez canal Shares. 1876.

Lord Lytton succeeded Lord Northbrook as Viceroy, and the Queen was proclaimed Empress Of India. Mr. Disraeli was created Earl of Beaconsfield, and Sir Stafford Northcote became leader of the House of Commons. An insurrection against Turkish oppression broke out in Bulgaria, and the atrocities that were there committed 4acited much indignati=in this country.

Captain Waring, of Latchford House, has received a: , :otter from Private G Kelly, of the Imperial Yeomanry,' who is one of the Warrington Ambulance Corns now on, duty in South Africa. It is dated " Imperial Yeomanry' Hospital, Deelfontein, Cape Colony." The writer says he had an attack of enteric before being made prisoner! at Lichtenburg. He was sent to Mafeking and Was in! bed when the celebrated tornada broke over the district ['heir teat was one of the first to be blown away, andi the gale took his blankets as well. He was over 20 minutes exposed to the storm. At first it was sand andl then rain in torrents. The wind was carrying tents and, kits about the camp like fury, and he lost all lois' kit and clothes except the one shirt he had on,and every man in hospital lost his kit. He never saw such a storm lbefore. The night was pitch dark, but they could see everything distinctly because the lightning flashed leearly every second. He was taken from there to the .Itation, and the following morning to the nrivate Victoria Hospital. He left there on September 2nd for Deelfontein, the Imperial Yeomanry Hospital, and • rrived there on the 4th. The good food and nursing ..00n brought him round, and he felt his own self again after having had pneumonia and enteric together and a ,relapse of dysentery. The doctor would not allow him, 'to go to the front again and he could not send him home, be put him on the staff as orderly. It was "orderlies irat and others after" here. The hospital is the best in both Africa. There is everything in it one can desire, .nd the patients are very well cared for. Captain Cros- ield is in hospital here also, convalescent from enteric.' Choy had a splendid dinner here on Christmas Day ml :the Imperial Yeomanry Theatre, and the sergeants and other non-crimmissioned officers waited upon them. EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

HATDOCIC PAILS RACES. Haydock Park steeplechases, Ist and 2nd 'February. For the convenience of the racing public !desirous of attending this steeplechase meeting the Great Central Railway Company announce in our 'advertising columns that they will run through 4trains from St Helens on each day running direct' to the Haydock Park (Racecourse Station) which' station adjoins the course. EMDEN DEATH OF A CHILD; The police have had reported to them the sudden' death of a child named Charles Ernest Parr, 61 months old. The child had been nursed by a Mrs' Howard of Bank-street, Earlestown, who has had, the care of it since it was two weeks old. It was, ways a weakly child and had been attended until a short time ago by Dr Gange. Shortly before 10 o'cleck on Friday the child was suddenly seized with convulsions and died before medical aid couldl be obtained. It is not expected that an inquest' will be held.

pial.

NARROW ESCAPE AT EAIILESTOWN STATION. On Saturday morning a porter named nankin had a very narraw escape from being killed at igarlestown station. He was wheeling a truck of greengrocery from ens platform to another by way of the level crossing at the end of the platform, when the Viaduct Works engine warned him that it lwas near. Rankin stopped at once, and the driver, thinking he was clear, came on, with the result that the engine step caught the truck, and pitched it clear off the rails, scattering the contents broad- cast. By some means Hankin's hand was caught' between the truck and the engine, and a severe . lacerated;wound was caused. FIRE AT EARLESTOWN. all'

Oa Friday afternoon a fire broke out in the Core, shed of Messrs. T. and T. Vicars's works at Ear covva, supposed to have been caused by the foundry =pole which it adjoins. The building being old ad dry the flames soon get a firm hold. Word was int to Messrs McCorquodale and Co., Newton, and their brigade quickly turned out,and after coupling up a stream of water was poured on to the flames. The pressure, however, was not very good and very little effect was produced. By dint of bard work the flames were finally got under and extinguished. rhe damage done is estimated at XlOO, the building being covered by insurance in the Norwich Union GRIMED YINTWIIEN BUFFERS AT EARLBSTOWN. On Saturday morning a young fellow named George Leigh employed as a painter at the Viaduct Works, was caught between the buffers of two wagons and crushed. He was werking between the wagons touching up the end of one of them. The works engine was engaged in shunting, and before putting other wagons aviinst those already in the siding, sounded a warning whistle, and Leigh came out clear of the wagons. The shunt was made,and Leigh thinking it was completed went between the t wagons again, Other wagons were sent in and Leigh was trapped. The accident was not seen by anyone, and nothing was known of it until Leigh was found lying across the rails. He has been medically attended, and is progressing as well as can be expected. ESCAPADE OF TWO BARLESTOWN BOYS. On Mondav,the parents of two youngsters named kiillard and Barlow were thrown into a state of great excitement, owing te the unaccountable absence of the lads who were only 11 years of age. Until shortly after eight o'clock in the morning, both lads were at home, but after that they could' not be found. When they did not turn up after dinner, the parents became really anxious and the aid of the police was sought. Enquiry was made by telephone in every direction, but without result and night closed in without any tidings of the truants. Next;morning the search was continued, but without success. The afternoon postal delivery brought a letter to Mr Millard, and the contents disclosed the fact that the runaways had reached the house of Mr Millard's mother at Sandbach,where they had arrived about half-past eight on Monday nignt. It is needless to say that the feelings of the nxious parents were very much relieved.

[LOYAL OLIVE BRANCH LODGE Cr ODDFELLOWS MANCHESTER UNION.

The annual meeting of this Lodge was held at the Green Dragon Inn on Thursday night last, under the presidency of PG Walter Flood. The Secretary (P P G M Alban Boyle) real the yearly balance sheet, which showed an increase in the funds to the end of December last year of .."7 163 11d. The amount received in contributions _donated to .£3OG 63 ; the total funds of the iJodge on the 31st December last being .£667 103 3d. After the usual business had been transacted, Past Grand Flood was presented with a framed past officer's certificate by PPGM Norman, on behalf of the officers and brethren of the Lodge, and in the course of his remraks said that he had always thought that the Noble Grand's services ought to be recognised in some way or other, and he some years ago suggested the presentation of these certificates, and he was glad t. say it had been carried out ever since. He thought it was an encouragement to young officers to throw more energy into their work. It was wonderful what good work could be done by young men iri a lodge like that to which they belonged, if they only put their shoulder to the wheel; He hoped that the young men of the lodge would aspire to take office —and so take a share in the good work connec- ted with the lodge. He believed that young men ought to belong to some order or another such the Manchester Unity, and it was a pleasure to him' to see so mans making provision tor the hour of .need which may overtake any of us. The object of these presentations was to induce young members of the lodge to take a keener interest in oidfellow- ship and he could say of P G Flood that he had known him from a youth and he had always found him most diligent in the duties he had to perform, and he felt sure that his efforts on behalf of his lodge had given every satisfaction and. te'hoped the presentation would remind him (P G Flood) of the many happy hours he had spent amongst his fellow oddfellows. P G Flood in acknowledging the presentation expressed his thankfulness for the kind expressions concerning himself and for the beautiful certificate of merit which they had been pleased to give him. He had tried to do his duty to the best of his ability and if in the future there was anything he could do for the benefit of odd- ellowship he would only be too glad to do it.

The Work of the Sunday School Teacher. On Monday evening the quarterly meeting of thei lunday School Teachers' Association was held in St. Inn's schoolroom, when an address upon "The pre- iaration of a lesson " was given by the Rev Morley keveuson. The Rev James Davies presided, and ithers present were the Revs EC E Carleton, E R Tarbuck, W Pollock-Hill, W Bracecarnp, G Vale 1 'Owen, W A Knight, and 0 W Dickenson.—Toe Rev 'A Stevenson said the work of a Sunday school teacher fell naturally into two important parts. `irst, of all there was the spiritual side of the work, and secondly there was the technizal side. Of those two undoubtedly the first was by far the more im- portant. However goad they might be in the tech- nicalities ot the subject, however skilled in the art of instruction, however they might have studied ithe science of education, unless they came as Sun- day school teachers by the help of God's Holy 'Spirit, in the spirit of prayer, with spiritual preparation completed, he ventured to think their lesson would not be very successful. He was not going to sneak to them of that most important part of the Sunday scholl teachers preparation. All-important though it might be, it was not for them to neglect the technical part of the work. As Sunday school teachers they were simply God's instrument, and it was their duty to itio all that in the.n lay to perfect the instrument for the work it had to accomplish. They could do so by the study ot the technical side of the teachers work. If they could make their lesson more fruit- ful, intelligent, intelligible, interesting and practical it would be wrong for them to neglect such oppor- tunities as these. To teach well or speak well they must prepare themselves very no, oughly. He had a very strong feeling in favour of what was called a preparation class. It they could only get their Sun- day school teachers under the guidance of the clergy to study the subject matter of the lesson that was going to be given throughout, the sThool,tbe teachers bringing forward their daculties and asking questions and the clergy explaining and pointing to fruitful lessons to be drawn from the various passages and illustrating with pictures, mans, diagrams, &c., he could not help thinking to at s'llli a meeting or class would tend very much to the improvement of the preparation of the lesson. The Chairman and the Revs EC C E Carleton and E R Tarbuck spoke appreciatively of Mr Stevenson's address, and on the motion of the Chairman the cordial thanks of the meeting were tendered to him,
