SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL

IDiscussion on the Lighting Question. A rneetine of the Great Sankey Parish Council wag !held in the Board School on Tuesday evening, Mr T Ilalton presiding over a full attendance. STMPATHY WITH THE ROYAL FAMILY. The Chairman said that before commencing the busi- aess of tha meeting be should like to mention the very - sad event of the sudden illness of their beloved Queen, and as Chairman he moved that the Council tendered their heartfelt symeathy with the members of the Royal Family. They knew that the minds of all must be centred, in view of the grave news received that morn- ing, upon the contemplation of the illness of her Majesty, who had had a long and pure reign, and had always had at heart the prosperity ot the people of the country. Mt Wriget seconded, and the resolution was carried in silence. I THE LIGHTING QIIIISTION—LAMPS TO UN EBECTID. The Clerk said be had written to Mr F (eider, &acre tary of the Corporation Gas Department, asking him for tbe cost per annum of eight lamps inclusive of lighting and extinguishing and the cost of the earns number of lamps exclusive of lighting and extinguishing. He had received a reply stating that the cost in the first instance would be 53e 9d per annum and in the second instance 38t. He hut also had corres- pondence with other gentlemen with reference to the lighting question. Mr. Cooks I propose that we leave the whole matter to the Rural District Council. The Chairman objected most stronelv to this proposi- tion and pointed out thet the parish of Sankey bad no: made any progressive strides fir a long tirue, espeeially with regard to the lighting question. The parish of Great Sankey was the only paean for a considerable dis- tance around Warrington whin was not sufficiently lighted. Mr Wright thouglat the laedoweers who would materi- ly benefit by the erection of lamps should bi asked to intribete towards the cost. Tee Chairman sail it was time they took steps in Ithe matter. If they:did not,the Rural District Council or some other equally powerful body would step in and enforce the erection of lamps. This would be putting a heavy debt upen the shoulders of the ratepayers. He proposed that the Council at once proceeded with the erection of nine lamps. Mr Wright seconded and the mo' ion was carried with only one dissentient (Mr Cook). It was decided to leave the question of lighting and extinguishing in the hands of the Rural District Council

THE PROSPEROUS GENERAL FURNISHERS. ON THE THRESHOLD OF A NEW YEAR, the 57th of PITTS'S Business Career, we deem it ad 'sable to scan past working in order to draw Inspiration - wuldanee for the future. As space permits but a brief .drospect, we refer only to what has been achieved since our advent in London-road. PITTS'S aspire and persevere. or, in homely language, we aim high and keep pegging at it. This, we believe, is the only royal road to success, the only path to prosperity, the one bee-line for the ambitious. We pursue it now and always have done, . with what results the following shows. I Rigby was employed under hit-n. The average weekly amount which he earned was between 203 and 233. Mr Scott delivered an able address bearing upon his contention. He argued that the shed was not a factory and the applicant when employed in the shed was not engaged in a business to which th 3 Act applied. There were a number of definitions of a factory, the sub- stantial fact of which was that a business of this kind could not constitute a factory unless it was carried on by the aid of machinery. In the netting shed itself there was no machinery and the machinery in the rope walk was not driven by mechanical power. Osatinuing, Mr Scott said that the geographical proximity of the shed dil not make it a factory. He submitted that a man who was employed during the day in a factory and worked overtime at night in the shed was in precisely the same position as if he went to a shed which was quarter of a mile away. Mr Browne said his contention was that they could not divide the works. If the works was a factory it was a factory as a whole. His Honour : There are certain essential qualifications which must exist :in order to make it a factory. Mr Leslie Scott says tinge conditions do not exist in this particular area and therefore it cannot be a factory. Mr. Browne : I say the whole works is a factory within the Factory and Workshops Act, and if anything wrong took place in the room there would be a convic- tion. If the shed were ow se the walls I should not argue one moment that it w. 6 I% factory. The hand netting is part and parcel of the business and you cannot separate one from the other for trio purpose of estimat- ing the earnings.

1577. The chief feature of this year was the war be- tween Russia and Turkey, which raged furiously in the Balkans for many months, and only ended in the fall of Plevna, after a heroic defence by Osman Pasha. 1878. This year will be sadly remembered by many as that in which the lamented Princess Alice died—a martyr to her maternal duty. The long and use- ful life of Earl Russell also came peaceably to a close. A Congress of the Great Powers was held at Berlin to endeavour to solve the Eastern problem, Lords Beaconsfield and Salisbury and Ode Russell being the representatives of England. 1879. The Zulu War—in which the Prince Imperial, Louis Napoleon, lost his life, in which a whole British force was annihilated at Isandhlwana, and which brought King Cetewayo a captive to Eng- land—belongs to this year. Peace was concluded with the Ameer, but afterwards the British Mission was foully massacred at Cabul. The Egyptian difficulty began by this time to loom up P • Ismail Pasha was deposed in favour of his son, Tewfik. At Windsor on March 13 the Duke of Con- naught was married to Princess Louise Margaret of Prussia. 1880. Parliament, after lasting six years was dissolved, and the result led to the Conservatives resigning and Lord Ilartington, Lord Granville, and Mr. Gladstone being successively sent for, Mr. Glad- stone consented to take office. The Queen paid a visit to the Continent, and opened Parliament in person. 1881. An Irish Land Bill, introduced by Mr. Glad- stone, and a bill for the protection of life and pro- perty in Ireland, were passed. On one occasion the House of Commons sat for 42 hours. Sir Wilfrid Lawson's Local Option Resolution was carried by 42 votes. April of this year witnessed the death of a great statesman in the person of Earl of Beaconsfield. On 13th March the startling news was received of the assassination of the Emperor of Russia. The war against Dutch Boers in the Transvaal resulted in a series of battles—in the last of which, that of Majuba Hill, our troops were terribly defeated—and a compromise for the sake of peace. 1882. The Egyptian ombroglio was now exciting great attention abroad. The state of affairs there resulted in the insurrection under Arabi Pasha, which was finally exterminated by British troops under General Wolseley. In the month of May Lord Frederick Cavendish and Mr. Burke were murdered in open day while walking in the Phcenie Park, Dublin, by Fenian& The world was this year again startled by another attempt on the Queen at Windsor. A man named Roderick Maclean fired a revolver at her Majesty as she drove past him from the Windsor railway station. The marriage of Prince Leopold to Princess Helen of Waldeck was one of the events of this year. 1883. The International Fisheries Exhibition at South Kensington was one of the features of this year, and its success led to several others in the follow- inn' years. A lump sum of £30,000 to Lord Wolseley and £25,000 to Lord Alcester was voted in recognition of their services in Egypt. The war in the Soudan became a source of ter- rible anxiety, and on September 6 an army of 10,000 strong was sent, under the command of Hicks Pasha by the Egyptian Government to attack the lLhdi. It was eneozmtered by the Arabs, attacked, defeated, and elataghtered. 1884. In this year a great trial fell upon the Queen in the sudden death of her youngest son, the Duke of Albany, which occurred at Cannes. The progress of the operations in the Soudan was the object of attention abroad, and the endeavours of Lord Wolseley at the head of a British expedition to rescue the heroic Genera Gordon immured .at Khartoum, were watched with breathless interest. The march, full of dangers, which poor, ill-fated General Stewart made across the desert from Korti, will not soon be forgotten. 1885. Early in the year the news came of Gorclon's death, causing the most widespread regret and moving the nation to tears. On 23rd July the marriage of Princess Beatrice with Prince Henry of Battenberg was celebrated at Whippingham Church, Isle of Wight. The Conservatives were returned in a majority at the General Election. 1886. An election took place in July,.wesulting in the return of the Conservatives to power. 1887-1897. The Jubilee of her Majesty's reign was cele- brated with great splendour in 1887,i In January, 1892, the Duke of Clarence (elder eon of the Prince of Wales) died fee& an attack of influenza. In 1893 the Duke of York was married to Princess May of Tack. In 1894 the Czar Alexander of Russia died and was succeeded by Nicholas 11., who married a granddaughter of the Queen. In the same year the wedding took place of the Grand Duke of Hesse and Princess Victoria Melita of Coburg. Her Majesty aroused great enthusiasm in Manchester by her visit thither for the purpose of formally opening the Ship Canal. In 1896 the Emperor Nicholas and the Empress arrived at Balmoral on a visit to the Queen. In January of this year Prince Henry of Battenburg (husband of the Princess Beatrice) died on his way home from Africa. In 1897 her Majesty completed the sixtieth year of her reign, and thus passed the longest limit pre- viously attained by any English sovereign. There were splendid pageants in London and celebrations throughout the length and breadth of the Empire. The presence of Colonial troops and represen- tatives of foreign states was a noteworthy feature, and the thanksgiving service at St. Paul's was a magnificent scene. 1897-1901. In 1899 the usual visit was made to the south of France in the spring and. her Majesty returned in May in time to lay the foundation stone of the Victoria and Albert Museum at South Kensington in the presence of . a brilliant assembly. The eightieth birthday of her Majesty was celebrated a few days afterwards both in London and the country, and throughout the Empire with impres- sive and enthusiastic. demonstrations of loyalty and affection. Later in the year she displayed wonder- ful vigour, both at her inspection of the Guards prior to their departure for South Africa, and on the occasion of a visit to Bristol. The usual visit to the Coniment, arranged for the spring of 1900, was abaudoned by her Majesty, whose care for her soldiers in South Africa was manifested in every possible way, as, for instance, by her gift to all the men out thereof "the Q ueen'sChocolate," originally designed as a Christmas gift, but unavoidably de- layed till after that date. On March Bth Her Majesty left Windsor and paid a short visit to London city. A huge crowd assembled in front of Buck- ingham Piteme at night and sang the National Anthem And other patriotic songs. On April 2 her Majesty I,ift Windsor Castle for Dublin, where she stayed lot three weeks. Her last previous visit to Ireland had been made 39 years before, and she received an enthusia.stic and even affec- tionate welcome from all ranks and creeds. Two heavy bereavements fell upon her Majesty during the year in the death of her son, Prince Alfred, Duke of Saxe-Coburg, and of her grandson, Prince Christian Victor, while on service in South Africa. In the early days of the present year her Majesty received Lord Roberts on his return from South Africa and created him an Earl and a K.O.
